CAT-VARC-P04-001 · History and memory · passage Medium · 440 words · 4 questions

Archives were not assembled to answer the questions historians now put to them. A colonial archive records what an administration needed to know: revenue collected and revenue owed, disorders suppressed, petitions received and disposed of. The people it governed appear in its pages chiefly at the moments when they became a problem for the state, as defaulters, suspects, rebels or supplicants. A historian who reads such records along the grain, accepting their categories as descriptions of reality, will end up reproducing the administration's own view of the world, in which a refusal to pay becomes 'obstinacy' and a gathering at a shrine becomes a 'disturbance of the peace'.

The remedy most often proposed is to read against the grain: to treat the record as evidence of what it was designed to suppress or dismiss. A report that a village had refused its dues 'without any cause' can be read for the cause the clerk declined to set down. The insistence of officials, repeated across a dozen letters, that a rumour was baseless tells us that the rumour mattered a great deal to them, and probably to those who spread it. Fear, in such documents, is a kind of evidence. Practised with care, this method has produced some of the most illuminating social history of recent generations, restoring motive and intelligence to people whom the record had reduced to nuisances.

Yet the method has a limit that its enthusiasts sometimes forget. Reading against the grain still reads the grain. The voices it recovers belong to those who left a trace by colliding with power; those who never collided, who paid what was asked, married, worked their fields and died without incident, remain exactly where the archive left them, outside it. There is also a quieter temptation. When the record is thin, the historian's sympathies rush in to fill it, and the rebel recovered from the margins of an official report can look suspiciously like the rebel the historian set out hoping to find.

None of this is a reason to abandon the method. It is a reason to be candid about what it can and cannot do. A silence in the archive is not always a hidden voice waiting to be decoded; sometimes it is simply an absence, and the honest response is to mark it as one. Beyond that, the historian can turn to sources that were not produced by the same gaze: remembered songs and stories, the remains of houses and tools, the shape of fields and paths. The archive can be cross-examined with great profit, but it cannot be made to testify about what it never saw.

CAT-VARC-P04-001-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · MediumMCQ
The primary purpose of the passage is to
  1. (A)explain how colonial clerks deliberately falsified reports in order to conceal the real causes of unrest from their superiors.
  2. (B)argue that the historians who read against the grain have mostly projected their own sympathies onto the people they study.
  3. (C)show that colonial archives are so distorted by official categories that historians should set them aside in favour of oral and material sources.
  4. (D)defend a way of reading official records while insisting that it has a boundary which honest historians must acknowledge.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 2 praises reading against the grain for restoring 'motive and intelligence' to the governed; paragraphs 3 and 4 set out its limit ('Reading against the grain still reads the grain') and conclude that silences must sometimes be marked as absences. The passage therefore defends the method while drawing its boundary.

Why not (A): The passage says clerks recorded events in the administration's categories and 'declined to set down' causes; it never claims deliberate falsification or concealment from superiors.

Why not (B): Paragraph 3 calls projection a 'quieter temptation', not the usual result, and paragraph 2 praises the method's achievements, so the claim that most such historians do this overstates the author's worry.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 says 'None of this is a reason to abandon the method' and that the archive 'can be cross-examined with great profit', so setting archives aside contradicts the author.

Common misconception: Seizing on the critical paragraphs and missing that the author explicitly keeps the method.

Difficulty: Two wrong options pick up the author's real worries but overstate them; the correct answer needs the praise and the limit held together. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-001-Q2 · Inference · EasyMCQ
The statement 'Fear, in such documents, is a kind of evidence' (paragraph 2) suggests that
  1. (A)the anxious repetition of an official denial can show that the thing denied mattered to those involved.
  2. (B)officials' fears about rumours were usually justified, since most rumours in colonial districts later proved to be true.
  3. (C)the people who spread rumours did so mainly in order to frighten the officials who governed them.
  4. (D)historians should rely on emotional language in documents rather than on figures such as revenue collected or owed.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

The preceding sentence explains the point: officials' insistence, 'repeated across a dozen letters', that a rumour was baseless 'tells us that the rumour mattered a great deal to them'. Official anxiety is thus read as evidence of significance.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 says the repeated denial shows the rumour 'mattered', not that it was true; whether rumours proved true is never discussed.

Why not (C): The motive of those who spread rumours is not given; the passage says only that the rumour probably mattered to them as well.

Why not (D): No ranking of emotional language over figures appears; the sentence concerns one kind of inference from official anxiety.

Common misconception: Turning 'mattered' into 'was true', or reading a motive into the rumour-spreaders.

Difficulty: The sentence just before the quoted phrase explains it directly. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-001-Q3 · Meaning in context · MediumMCQ
Which one of the following best captures the meaning of 'Reading against the grain still reads the grain' as used in paragraph 3?
  1. (A)A critical reading of official records must begin with an uncritical reading, so that the historian first understands what the officials meant.
  2. (B)Historians who claim to read against the grain usually end up accepting the administration's categories without noticing that they have done so.
  3. (C)However critically the records are read, the method can only work with the people and events that those records happened to capture.
  4. (D)Records produced by the state are the only reliable source available for the lives of ordinary people under colonial rule.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

The sentence that follows explains the phrase: the method recovers only voices 'who left a trace by colliding with power', while those who never collided 'remain exactly where the archive left them, outside it'. The grain, the records themselves, still fixes what can be found.

Why not (A): The paragraph is about who is missing from the record, not about the stages of reading; nothing describes a preliminary uncritical reading.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 credits the method with genuinely restoring motive to the governed, so the author does not think it collapses back into accepting official categories.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 recommends other sources, such as songs and the remains of houses, so the author does not treat state records as the only reliable source.

Common misconception: Taking the phrase as a charge that the method fails, rather than as a statement of its limit.

Difficulty: The first wrong option is a plausible reading of the words that the rest of the passage rules out. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-001-Q4 · Application / analogy · DifficultMCQ
A historian studying a colonial district finds that its records say almost nothing about the farm work done by women. Which response would the author of the passage most likely endorse?
  1. (A)Generalising about all the district's women from the few court cases in which women appear as defendants or as petitioners.
  2. (B)Stating plainly that the records are silent, and turning to evidence such as surviving tools, field layouts and remembered work songs.
  3. (C)Treating the silence itself as proof that officials deliberately suppressed women's resistance, and reconstructing that resistance from it.
  4. (D)Concluding that because the district's records rarely mention it, women's farm work was probably a minor part of the local economy.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 4 says that a silence may be 'simply an absence', that the honest response is 'to mark it as one', and that the historian can turn to sources not produced by the same gaze, naming 'remembered songs', 'tools' and 'the shape of fields'. The correct answer applies exactly this advice.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 says the voices recovered belong to those who collided with power; generalising from them to those who never collided is the error the author describes.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 warns that a silence 'is not always a hidden voice waiting to be decoded', and paragraph 3 warns against filling a thin record with the historian's sympathies.

Why not (D): This reads along the grain, treating the archive's categories as 'descriptions of reality', which paragraph 1 says reproduces the administration's view.

Common misconception: Choosing the option that sounds most critical of the archive (deliberate suppression), which the author treats as a temptation rather than a method.

Difficulty: Each wrong option applies a different error the passage describes; the solver must map all three and see that only the correct answer follows paragraph 4. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P04-002 · History and memory · passage Easy · 463 words · 4 questions

Arguments about public statues tend to be conducted as if there were only two positions. On one side are those who say that the statue of a figure now held in disrepute should come down, because a city ought not to go on honouring what it has come to find shameful. On the other are those who say that removing it 'erases history', and that a mature society learns from its past instead of hiding it. The quarrel is loud, and it is often bitter, because each side suspects the other of bad faith: of wishing to rewrite the past, or of wishing to keep its injustices on a pedestal. Each side assumes it knows what a statue is. Neither, I think, quite does.

A statue is not a history lesson. It records almost nothing about the life of its subject: a name, a pair of dates, perhaps a line of praise. What it records very precisely is a decision, taken at a particular moment by particular people, to honour that subject in that place. Many of the statues now in dispute were raised long after the events they commemorate, often by committees with purposes of their own, such as asserting the standing of a group or a cause at a time when that standing was being questioned. A statue is therefore better evidence about the people who raised it than about the person it depicts.

Seen in this light, the charge of erasing history loses much of its force. Taking a statue down does not alter what happened, and the books, records and family memories that tell its subject's story remain where they were. What removal changes is a present act of honour, and every generation is entitled to reconsider whom it honours. Yet the other camp should not feel wholly vindicated. A statue that is quietly carted away also removes evidence of what an earlier generation chose to celebrate, and that evidence can be uncomfortable in useful ways, reminding a city that the values it now rejects were once publicly and confidently held by respectable people.

The popular compromise of leaving the statue in place and adding an explanatory plaque is often presented as the neutral option. It is not. A plaque is also an editorial act: someone must decide what it says, how long it is and whose judgement it expresses, and a few lines of small print beneath a towering bronze figure rarely win the argument with it. Moving a statue to a museum, where the date and purpose of its making can be displayed alongside it, may come closer to keeping the evidence without continuing the honour. Whatever a community decides, it should at least be clear that it is deciding how to honour, not whether to remember.

CAT-VARC-P04-002-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, what a public statue records most precisely is
  1. (A)a choice, made by certain people at a certain time, to honour its subject in that place.
  2. (B)the settled judgement of an entire community about the moral worth of the person it depicts.
  3. (C)the events that its subject took part in, as they were understood when those events occurred.
  4. (D)the main achievements of its subject, set down in a form that passers-by can easily read.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 2 states: 'What it records very precisely is a decision, taken at a particular moment by particular people, to honour that subject in that place.' The correct answer paraphrases this sentence.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 attributes the decision to 'particular people', often committees with their own purposes, not to an entire community's settled judgement.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 notes that many statues were raised 'long after the events they commemorate', so they do not record how the events were understood at the time.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says a statue records 'almost nothing about the life of its subject', only a name, dates and perhaps a line of praise.

Common misconception: Assuming that a statue records its subject's life or a community-wide verdict.

Difficulty: Located in a single sentence of paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-002-Q2 · Structure / function · EasyMCQ
The discussion of the explanatory plaque in paragraph 4 serves mainly to
  1. (A)show that plaques are the best available means of preserving a statue's value as historical evidence.
  2. (B)challenge the assumption that keeping a statue and adding text beside it is a choice free of anyone's judgement.
  3. (C)illustrate how committees in the past used inscriptions to assert the standing of a group or cause.
  4. (D)argue that statues of figures held in disrepute should always be removed rather than explained.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 4 opens by saying the plaque 'is often presented as the neutral option. It is not.' It then explains that a plaque 'is also an editorial act', requiring someone to decide what it says and whose judgement it expresses.

Why not (A): Paragraph 4 says a museum 'may come closer to keeping the evidence', so the author does not rank plaques as the best means.

Why not (C): The plaque in paragraph 4 is a present-day compromise; the committees asserting standing appear in paragraph 2 and concern the statues themselves.

Why not (D): The author never says statues should always be removed; paragraph 3 warns that removal also loses useful evidence.

Common misconception: Taking the plaque discussion as an endorsement of plaques, or linking it to the historical committees.

Difficulty: The function is announced in the first two sentences of the paragraph. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-002-Q3 · Inference · MediumMCQ
It can be inferred that the author regards the claim that removing a statue 'erases history' as
  1. (A)correct only for statues raised soon after the events they depict, since later statues have no value as evidence of anything.
  2. (B)beside the point, because statues contain no evidence of any kind and their removal therefore costs a society nothing at all.
  3. (C)mistaken about what is lost, since removal ends an act of honour and some evidence of past values, not knowledge of the subject.
  4. (D)broadly correct, because a statue is the main means by which most ordinary citizens come to learn about the leading figures of their nation's past.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 3 says the charge 'loses much of its force' because removal 'does not alter what happened' and the sources that tell the subject's story remain. But it adds that removal 'also removes evidence of what an earlier generation chose to celebrate'. The author thus thinks the charge misidentifies the loss without thinking removal costs nothing.

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 says later statues are good evidence 'about the people who raised it', so the claim that they have no evidential value is false to the passage.

Why not (B): Paragraph 3 says a removed statue 'also removes evidence of what an earlier generation chose to celebrate', so the author thinks removal has a real cost.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says a statue 'is not a history lesson' and records almost nothing about its subject, which contradicts this.

Common misconception: Picking the option that rejects the charge outright, missing the author's concession in paragraph 3.

Difficulty: The tempting wrong option matches the author's rejection of the charge but ignores the concession. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-002-Q4 · Except / not supported · MediumMCQ
Each of the following is consistent with the views expressed in the passage EXCEPT:
  1. (A)The wording of an explanatory plaque reflects the judgement of whoever was given the task of writing it.
  2. (B)A statue raised long after its subject died may reveal more about its builders' concerns than about its subject.
  3. (C)A generation may legitimately decide to stop honouring a figure whom an earlier generation, with purposes of its own, chose to honour.
  4. (D)A statue moved from a public square into a museum gallery goes on honouring its subject much as it did before.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 4 says a museum, which displays the date and purpose of a statue's making, 'may come closer to keeping the evidence without continuing the honour'. The claim that the moved statue goes on honouring its subject as before contradicts this.

Why not (A): Paragraph 4 says someone must decide 'whose judgement it expresses', calling the plaque 'an editorial act', so this is consistent.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 says such statues are 'better evidence about the people who raised it than about the person it depicts', so this is consistent.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 says 'every generation is entitled to reconsider whom it honours', so this is consistent with the passage.

Common misconception: Overlooking the phrase 'without continuing the honour' and assuming relocation changes nothing.

Difficulty: Three statements are paraphrases of the passage; the exception contradicts one clause in the last paragraph. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-003 · History and memory · passage Medium · 444 words · 4 questions

Most national histories begin with a land and a people presented as though they had always been there, waiting to become a nation. Kings who reigned many centuries earlier, battles fought for reasons that had nothing to do with any modern state, and poets who wrote in dialects their supposed descendants can barely read are enlisted as the opening chapters of a single story whose last chapter is the present. Rulers who owed allegiance to dynasties spread across several of today's countries, and who would not have recognised the modern map, are recast as founders. The past is read backwards from its outcome and then retold forwards as if the outcome had been its purpose all along.

Critics call this anachronism, and on the main charge they are right. But the criticism is often pressed further than it can go, into the claim that because nations are modern creations, the materials of national history are simply invented. That does not follow. Languages, forms of worship, routes of trade and shared songs long predated modern states, and they plainly did shape which communities later found it easy, and which found it hard, to imagine themselves as one. The national historian's mistake is not to notice these continuities. It is to arrange them as stages on the way to an end that was fixed in advance.

The distinction matters because the arrangement has consequences. If the nation is the hero of the story, then whatever the past contained that does not fit the hero's career must be either left out or cast as a failure of loyalty. Regions that resisted unification, communities whose ties ran across the eventual border, rival projects that might have produced a different state or several smaller ones: all of these become obstacles overcome rather than possibilities that once existed and were, for a time, taken seriously by the people who lived among them. A province that held out for its own arrangements appears only as a delay, and a language that lost out to the standard tongue appears, if at all, as a quaint survival. The coherence of the story is purchased by narrowing its cast.

A better history would keep the continuities and restore the alternatives. It would ask what other political communities were possible at each point, what the people of the time thought they were choosing between, and why this one prevailed, treating the result as an outcome to be explained rather than a destiny to be celebrated. Such a history may make for less stirring lessons in school. It is also far less likely to make citizens strangers to whole regions and peoples of their own past.

CAT-VARC-P04-003-Q1 · Author's tone / attitude · EasyMCQ
The author's attitude towards the critics mentioned at the start of paragraph 2 is best described as
  1. (A)studied neutrality: reporting their charge without indicating whether it is persuasive.
  2. (B)dismissive disagreement: treating their charge of anachronism as a basic misunderstanding of how history is actually written.
  3. (C)unreserved support: endorsing both their charge and the claim that national history is simply invented.
  4. (D)qualified agreement: accepting their central charge while rejecting one conclusion some of them draw from it.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 2 says 'on the main charge they are right' and then 'But the criticism is often pressed further than it can go', rejecting the inference that national history's materials are invented. That is agreement with a stated limit.

Why not (A): The author explicitly evaluates the critics, agreeing on the main charge and saying the criticism 'is often pressed further than it can go'.

Why not (B): The author says that 'on the main charge they are right', which rules out dismissive disagreement.

Why not (C): The author says the claim that the materials are 'simply invented' does not follow, so the support has reservations.

Common misconception: Missing the 'But' and reading the paragraph as either full agreement or rejection.

Difficulty: The attitude is stated in two consecutive sentences. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-003-Q2 · Inference · MediumMCQ
The passage suggests that the 'coherence' of a national history of the kind it criticises is achieved chiefly by
  1. (A)omitting or condemning those parts of the past that pointed towards other political outcomes.
  2. (B)relying on poets and chroniclers who were themselves committed to the idea of a unified nation.
  3. (C)describing how rival political projects were peacefully absorbed into the nation once it was formed.
  4. (D)tracing real continuities of language, worship and trade over many centuries of the region's history.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 3 says whatever does not fit 'must be either left out or cast as a failure of loyalty' and concludes that 'The coherence of the story is purchased by narrowing its cast.' The regions, communities and rival projects it lists are all alternatives to the eventual nation.

Why not (B): Paragraph 1 says the poets wrote in dialects their descendants can barely read; nothing says they were committed to national unity.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 says such projects become 'obstacles overcome' or are left out; nothing is said about peaceful absorption.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says noticing continuities is not the mistake; paragraph 3 attributes the coherence to narrowing the cast, not to the continuities themselves.

Common misconception: Choosing the continuities option, because the passage does accept them, without noticing that the author separates them from the source of coherence.

Difficulty: The first wrong option uses the author's own accepted point in the wrong role. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-003-Q3 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following, if true, would most weaken the author's claim that older continuities shaped which communities later found it easy to imagine themselves as one?
  1. (A)Some medieval rulers spoke several languages and held lands in regions that now belong to two or three different modern countries.
  2. (B)National histories in many countries trace the nation back to a legendary founder whose actual existence cannot be confirmed from any surviving written or archaeological source.
  3. (C)Studies of several regions found that national feeling formed just as readily within borders that cut across older zones of shared language, worship and trade.
  4. (D)Many modern states, once formed, required a single standard language in their schools and steadily suppressed regional dialects in all official business.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 2 claims that languages, worship, trade and songs 'plainly did shape which communities later found it easy, and which found it hard, to imagine themselves as one'. If national feeling formed just as readily across those older zones as within them, the continuities made no difference to which communities could imagine themselves as one, and the claim is undercut. The other options concern a different claim or a later period.

Why not (A): This matches paragraph 1's point about rulers recast as founders and is consistent with the author; it does not bear on whether older continuities shaped later communities.

Why not (B): This supports the charge of backward projection in paragraph 1; it is silent on whether real continuities shaped later national communities.

Why not (D): This describes what states did after they formed; it does not show that older continuities failed to shape which communities could imagine themselves as one.

Common misconception: Choosing an option that criticises national histories, and so feels like it opposes something in the passage, without checking which claim it bears on.

Difficulty: All options concern nationalism; the solver must isolate one specific causal claim and test each option against it. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P04-003-Q4 · Structure / function · MediumMCQ
Which of the following best describes the structure of the passage?
  1. (A)A criticism of national history is stated, the criticism is rejected as anachronistic, examples of its errors are given, and national history is defended.
  2. (B)A way of writing history is described, a criticism of it is accepted but limited, the harm of the error is traced, and an alternative proposed.
  3. (C)Two rival accounts of the origins of nations are compared, evidence for each is weighed, and the author concludes that both are partly mistaken.
  4. (D)A historical example is narrated in detail, a general principle is drawn from it, and the principle is applied to the teaching of history in schools.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 1 describes the practice of reading the past backwards from the nation; paragraph 2 accepts the charge of anachronism but rejects the claim that the materials are invented; paragraph 3 explains the consequences of the teleological arrangement; paragraph 4 proposes a history that 'would keep the continuities and restore the alternatives'.

Why not (A): The author agrees with the critics' main charge in paragraph 2 and ends by proposing a different kind of history, not by defending national history.

Why not (C): The passage does not set out two accounts of national origins; it discusses one way of writing history and one criticism of it.

Why not (D): No single example is narrated; paragraph 1 describes a general practice, and schools are mentioned only in passing in paragraph 4.

Common misconception: Missing the limited agreement in paragraph 2, or treating the brief mention of schools as the passage's destination.

Difficulty: Requires following all four paragraphs and recognising the partial concession. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-004 · History and memory · passage Easy · 401 words · 4 questions

Historians trained on written records have long treated oral testimony with suspicion, and their reasons are not foolish. Memories fade and are reshaped by everything that happened afterwards; a speaker recalling events of fifty years ago has also absorbed fifty years of conversation, newspapers and family retelling, and cannot always tell what she saw from what she later heard. Anyone who has compared an elderly person's account of a local event with the surviving paperwork knows how often the dates, the sequence and even the participants differ. As a chronicle of what happened and when, the spoken memory of a single witness is frequently unreliable.

The objection assumes, however, that the only purpose of testimony is to establish what happened. Consider a hypothetical mining town in which many older residents, interviewed separately, place a bitter strike in the same year as a disastrous flood, although the records show that the two events were several years apart. As evidence about dates, these accounts are simply wrong. As evidence about how the town came to understand its own past, they are remarkably revealing, for the merging of the two events suggests that residents experienced them as a single catastrophe, perhaps because in both the company that owned the mine was felt to have abandoned them.

The crucial point is that shared and patterned errors are not the same as noise. Individual slips of memory are scattered more or less at random; one witness misplaces a year, another confuses two neighbours. When many people independently make the same mistake, however, the mistake becomes a social fact, something that calls for explanation rather than mere correction. And patterned error of this kind is precisely what written records cannot supply, because documents tend to record events, while the meanings that people attached to those events, and the way those meanings settled over decades, rarely find their way into an official file.

None of this licenses the historian to treat every inaccuracy as meaningful or to abandon the ordinary work of checking. An error can be interpreted only once it has been established that it is an error, and that usually requires the very documents that oral historians are sometimes too quick to dismiss. The strongest work therefore uses the two kinds of source together, letting each expose what the other cannot see: the record correcting the memory, and the memory revealing what the record left out.

CAT-VARC-P04-004-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, a mistake made independently by many witnesses differs from an individual slip of memory in that it
  1. (A)becomes a feature of shared understanding that needs to be explained rather than merely corrected.
  2. (B)can be corrected more easily, because the written records usually show where the witnesses went wrong.
  3. (C)is less likely to be an error at all, since agreement among witnesses usually indicates that they are right.
  4. (D)reveals the date of the event more reliably than an official document written at the time could do.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 3 contrasts individual slips, 'scattered more or less at random', with the same mistake made by many people, which 'becomes a social fact, something that calls for explanation rather than mere correction'.

Why not (B): Paragraph 3 does not say shared mistakes are easier to correct; it says they call 'for explanation rather than mere correction'.

Why not (C): In the mining-town case the shared dating is 'simply wrong' about dates, so agreement does not make the account accurate.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says 'As evidence about dates, these accounts are simply wrong'; their value lies in meanings, not in dating.

Common misconception: Assuming that agreement among witnesses must make them right.

Difficulty: Located in two adjacent sentences of paragraph 3. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-004-Q2 · Meaning in context · EasyMCQ
As used in paragraph 3, the phrase 'a social fact' most nearly means
  1. (A)an event that is confirmed by both oral testimony and official written records.
  2. (B)a falsehood that a community spreads deliberately in order to mislead outsiders.
  3. (C)something true of the community's shared outlook, as distinct from a single person's lapse.
  4. (D)a statistic about how often older people make errors when recalling dates.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

The phrase contrasts the shared mistake with individual slips 'scattered more or less at random'. Because it is shared, it reflects how the community understood events and 'calls for explanation', so it is a fact about the group rather than about any one memory.

Why not (A): The shared mistake is contradicted by the records, so 'social fact' cannot mean an event that the records confirm.

Why not (B): The witnesses err 'independently', and the passage gives no hint that the mistake is deliberate or aimed at outsiders.

Why not (D): The phrase describes one shared mistake that calls for explanation, not a general rate of error among the elderly.

Common misconception: Reading 'fact' as 'true event' and missing that the fact is the shared mistake itself.

Difficulty: The meaning follows from the contrast in the same paragraph. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-004-Q3 · Inference · MediumMCQ
The author would most likely agree that, in the mining-town case, the records showing when the strike and the flood occurred
  1. (A)are of little further use to the historian once the residents' interviews have been recorded and analysed.
  2. (B)are needed to establish that the residents' dating is mistaken before that mistake can be interpreted.
  3. (C)should be doubted, since so many residents interviewed separately agreed on a different account of the dates.
  4. (D)show how the residents felt about the mine's owners during the years between the two events.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 4 says 'An error can be interpreted only once it has been established that it is an error, and that usually requires the very documents' oral historians may dismiss. In the mining-town case, the records are what show that the strike and flood were years apart.

Why not (A): Paragraph 4 says the strongest work uses both kinds of source together, 'the record correcting the memory', so the records stay useful.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 says the residents' accounts are 'simply wrong' about dates, so the author trusts the records on that point.

Why not (D): Paragraph 3 says documents 'tend to record events' while the meanings people attached to them rarely reach an official file.

Common misconception: Choosing the option that credits documents with revealing feelings, which the passage assigns to testimony.

Difficulty: Requires applying the general point in paragraph 4 to the case in paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-005 · History and memory · passage Difficult · 438 words · 4 questions

Many historians regard the question 'what if?' as a parlour game, an amusement for the armchair strategist that has no place in serious scholarship. The past, they say, happened only one way, and the historian's task is to explain that way, not to speculate about others for which no evidence could ever exist. The objection has the appeal of modesty, but it rests on a misunderstanding of what historians already do. To say that a harvest failure caused a revolt is to say, at the very least, that without the failure the revolt would not have occurred when and as it did, or would have been less likely. Every causal claim carries such a counterfactual inside it, and the historian who refuses to examine counterfactuals has not avoided them; he has merely declined to inspect the ones he is already using.

The real question, then, is not whether to reason counterfactually but how to do it with discipline. Two constraints seem essential. The first is that the altered antecedent should be minimal and plausible in its own time: the most useful alternatives are those that people at the moment actually contemplated, such as a decision that was debated and narrowly taken, rather than those that require rewriting the character of a ruler or the technology of an age. The second is that the horizon of consequences should be short. Each step away from the altered moment multiplies the contingencies in play, and within a few years the alternative world becomes as indeterminate as the future, which is why the popular genre of imagining a whole century transformed by one changed battle is closer to fiction than to history.

A more searching objection holds that even a disciplined counterfactual smuggles in assumptions about how societies behave, about which pressures are strong and which choices are free, that cannot themselves be tested. This is true, but it is not peculiar to counterfactuals. The ordinary causal explanation rests on exactly the same assumptions; it simply leaves them unstated. What the explicit counterfactual offers is not freedom from such assumptions but exposure of them, so that a reader can see what the explanation depends on and dispute it.

Used this way, the counterfactual is less a tool for discovering what would have happened than a tool for testing what is claimed to have been bound to happen. Historians and politicians alike are fond of declaring outcomes inevitable. To show that a serious alternative was open, contemplated and within reach at the time is enough to defeat that claim, even when nothing reliable can be said about where the alternative would have led.

  3. (C)show that an ordinary causal claim already contains a counterfactual within it.
  4. (D)suggest that historians usually overstate the importance of single events such as failed harvests.
Answer and explanation

Immediately after the example the author says: 'Every causal claim carries such a counterfactual inside it', concluding that the historian who rejects counterfactuals 'has not avoided them'. The example illustrates that point.

Why not (A): The author uses the example against the critics of counterfactuals; it is an ordinary causal claim, not idle speculation.

Why not (B): The example illustrates the logic of causal claims; the passage makes no general claim about the causes of revolts.

Why not (D): Nothing in paragraph 1 says historians overstate single causes; the example is about what any causal claim implies.

Common misconception: Assuming that any 'what if' example must be one the author disapproves of.

Difficulty: The function is stated in the sentence after the example. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-006 · History and memory · passage Medium · 402 words · 4 questions

The names we give to periods of the past look like neutral conveniences, markers on a timeline that allow us to say roughly when something happened. In fact most of them are compressed arguments. 'The Middle Ages' is the clearest case. The phrase took hold among European writers who believed that they were reviving the learning of the ancient world after a long interruption, and who therefore saw the intervening thousand years as an interval, a middle that mattered chiefly as the gap between two moments of achievement. A period named in this way has been judged before it has been studied.

Generations of historians have since shown how little that judgement fits the evidence. The centuries in question saw the founding of universities, the building of great cathedrals, the elaboration of law and the slow growth of towns and trade; far from being an interruption, they laid much of the ground on which later achievements stood. Yet the name has survived the demolition of the view that produced it, and it continues to whisper that the period was something to be got through. Words outlast the arguments that coined them.

The difficulty grows when such a label is exported. To speak of a 'medieval' period in the history of a region far from Europe is to import a chronology built for one part of the world, with its own sense of what came before and after, and to invite the historian to look there for whatever the label suggests, such as a decline and a later rebirth, whether or not the evidence of that region points to anything of the kind.

It does not follow that we should try to do without period names. History cannot be written without some way of grouping years together, and the alternatives are not innocent either: centuries are as arbitrary as any label, cutting through lives and movements at points chosen by arithmetic, and they bring a peculiar habit of their own, the tendency to treat each hundred years as having a single character. The sensible course is to keep the names while holding them lightly, treating each as a hypothesis about what a stretch of time had in common, to be tested against the evidence and abandoned where it fails. The danger lies not in having labels but in letting the label arrive before the research and decide what the research will look for.

Why not (B): The writers saw the period as an interval after ancient achievement, not as a decline that began before the ancient world peaked.

Common misconception: Attributing the later historians' view to the writers who coined the name.

Difficulty: The answer is stated in one sentence; one wrong option is drawn from the next paragraph. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-007 · History and memory · passage Medium · 600 words · 5 questions

Among professional historians it has become something of a reflex to sneer at heritage. The restored cottage with its costumed guide, the festival that re-enacts a battle every summer, the gift shop selling replica coins and the television drama in which everyone has perfect teeth are all, on this view, so many falsifications: a past made comfortable, sold by the ticket and stripped of everything that might disturb a family on a day out. The critics are not wrong about the facts. Heritage does select, simplify and flatter, and it usually offers the past with the smell taken out.

What the critique misses is that heritage is not attempting to be history and failing at it. The two draw on the same materials, the same buildings, objects and stories, but they put them to different uses. History asks questions of the past and follows the evidence wherever it leads, including towards conclusions that unsettle the people who hear them. Heritage asks the past to confirm something about the present: that this town has deep roots, that this people has endured, that the place where one lives is worth caring about. Its characteristic question is not 'what happened here, and why?' but 'why should this place and its people matter to us?', and its characteristic answer is an experience rather than an argument. It is closer to commemoration than to inquiry, and it should be judged as commemoration is judged.

Consider a family that marks a grandparent's long marriage with a party, old photographs on the walls and affectionate speeches. Nobody expects the speeches to include an account of the couple's worst quarrels, and nobody thinks the omission a lie; the occasion exists to celebrate, and everyone present understands this. The tidying that heritage performs is, in the same way, not itself the offence. A restored village that shows thatched roofs and spinning wheels but not the open drains or the cramped rooms where servants slept is doing what celebrations do. There is even a case that it does history a service, for many people first sense that the past was inhabited by real people when they stand in such a room, and the curiosity awakened there leads some of them on to the harder questions that history asks.

The trouble begins at a specific point, and it is worth locating precisely. It comes when heritage claims the authority of history: when a site presents its selective story not as a celebration but as an account of how things really were, when a guidebook dismisses awkward evidence as the invention of cynics, or when public money that once sustained archives and libraries is redirected to attractions that will draw larger crowds. A museum label that calls a disputed episode 'settled', or a re-enactment whose script quietly drops the side that lost, does more than simplify; it borrows the standing of scholarship in order to close a question that scholarship has left open. At that point celebration has stopped being one use of the past among others and has begun to crowd out the use that tests it.

The sensible relation between the two is neither contempt nor merger. History should remain the critic of heritage, ready to point out what a display leaves out and to say so in public; heritage can remain what it is, a doorway through which many people first enter the past. The line that needs defending is not the one between the professional and the popular, but the one between celebration that knows itself to be celebration and celebration that presents itself as knowledge.

  4. (D)Heritage and history are in the end the same activity, since both make use of the same buildings, objects and stories from the past.

Common misconception: Taking the historians' sneer in paragraph 1 as the author's own view, or treating one example as the thesis.

CAT-VARC-P04-008 · History and memory · passage Difficult · 579 words · 5 questions

Few moral injunctions are now repeated with more confidence than the injunction to remember. After a civil war, a dictatorship or a period of communal violence, it is taken almost for granted that the truth must be told, the dead named and the guilty identified, and that a society which declines to do so is building its future on a lie. The case for this view is strong, and nothing that follows is meant to deny it. Those who suffered have a claim to be believed, and a society that teaches its children a false account of its own recent past does them a lasting harm. But the view carries an assumption that deserves examination: that remembering, in public and in detail, is always what allows former enemies to go on living side by side.

The historical record is less obliging than the injunction. A number of societies, ancient and modern, have made peace by agreeing, in one form or another, not to reopen the wrongs of the conflict just ended. Such agreements are often described as attempts at forgetting, but the description is misleading. No decree can make people forget what they suffered or what they did, and none of these arrangements tried to. What they established was something narrower: a rule about the public use of the past. Past crimes would not be prosecuted, or not beyond a certain point; old grievances would not be made the basis of political campaigns; the question of who had done what to whom would, for a time, be kept out of the courts and the assemblies, even as it continued to be remembered in kitchens and graveyards.

Whether such a rule is wise seems to depend less on the principle involved than on timing. In the first years after a conflict, when the losing side still has the means to fight and the institutions of the new order are fragile, a full public reckoning may reopen the war it is meant to close, and the people most likely to pay for that are the ones a reckoning was supposed to vindicate. A generation later, the balance may look quite different. The armed men have grown old, the institutions have settled, and the children of those who suffered may reasonably ask why the silence that once protected the peace should still protect those who did the harm.

For the costs of silence do not fall evenly. The perpetrator who is spared prosecution gains something definite; the victim who is asked not to speak of what was done gives something up, and is often asked to do so by people who lost far less. A peace that rests indefinitely on such an asymmetry is unlikely to remain a peace in any full sense. It may endure as an arrangement while decaying as a settlement, leaving resentments that later politicians find all too easy to exploit.

The upshot is that agreed silence should be judged neither as a betrayal of memory nor as a model of wisdom, but as a provisional arrangement whose legitimacy depends on what happens to it later. A silence that a society chooses, and later chooses to break, may have served both peace and justice in their turn. The worst outcome is not silence as such but a silence that becomes permanent by default, that no one ever decides to keep, and that is mistaken, by those who benefit from it, for a consensus that was never actually reached.

CAT-VARC-P04-009 · Empire and its aftermath · passage Easy · 456 words · 4 questions

Arguments about the legacy of empire often take the form of an account book. On one side of the ledger are the railways, telegraphs, irrigation canals and universities that colonial governments built; on the other are the drained revenues, the famines, the suppressed industries and the humiliations of foreign rule. Defenders of empire add up the first column and find a surplus; its critics add up the second and find a debt. The exchange can continue indefinitely, because each side chooses what to count, and because neither can say what a fair price for a railway would be.

The deeper difficulty is that the ledger treats the items in the first column as gifts, things handed over to a population that would otherwise have lacked them. Consider the railway. In much of the colonial world, lines were laid out to connect mines, plantations and cotton-growing districts to the ports, and to move soldiers quickly to places where unrest might break out. Routes, gauges and freight rates were set according to the needs of export trade and military control, and the cost of construction was frequently met from local taxation or guaranteed by it. A railway designed in this way is not a present; it is an investment made by one party, with another party's money, for the first party's purposes. To enter it as a credit in the colonised society's account is to confuse being affected by something with being given it.

None of this means that colonial railways were useless to the people who lived alongside them. Passengers, often in numbers that surprised the companies, used the trains to visit distant shrines, to look for work in growing cities, to carry goods to new markets and, eventually, to travel to political meetings that the authorities would have preferred them not to attend. A technology built for one end was turned to others. But this point cuts against the ledger rather than in its favour, since the uses that mattered most to ordinary people were precisely the ones the builders had not intended and could not claim as their contribution.

What the account-book approach leaves out, then, is the question of who decided. A society that chooses to borrow money and build a railway, and gets the routes wrong, has made its own mistake and can learn from it. A society that has a railway built for it, on terms set elsewhere, has acquired a piece of infrastructure but not the habit of deciding about infrastructure. That second loss does not appear in any column, which is perhaps why the ledger has proved so durable: it allows both its defenders and its critics to argue about outcomes while ignoring the matter of agency altogether.

CAT-VARC-P04-009-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · MediumMCQ
The primary purpose of the passage is to
  1. (A)argue that unplanned uses of colonial railways by ordinary people show that the benefits of empire outweighed its costs.
  2. (B)demonstrate that, taken as a whole, colonial railways did more harm than good to the societies through which their lines happened to run.
  3. (C)explain why colonial governments chose railway routes that served export trade and military control rather than local need.
  4. (D)show that weighing empire as a ledger misleads, since imposed works are not gifts and the question of who decided is left out.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 1 introduces the ledger debate, paragraph 2 argues that its credits are not gifts, paragraph 3 concedes real local use but turns it against the ledger, and paragraph 4 names what the ledger omits: 'the question of who decided'. The purpose is to expose the flaws in the ledger approach itself.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 says these uses cut 'against the ledger rather than in its favour', so the option reverses the author's point.

Why not (B): The author never totals the columns; paragraph 1 says the exchange 'can continue indefinitely', and the passage declines to settle it either way.

Why not (C): Route choice is one illustration in paragraph 2; it supports the argument about gifts but is far too narrow to be the purpose.

Common misconception: Taking the author to be on the critics' side of the ledger, or mistaking the railway illustration for the point.

Difficulty: The reader must see that the author rejects the framing rather than joining one side; two wrong options take a side and one is a detail. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-010 · Empire and its aftermath · passage Medium · 465 words · 4 questions

It has become common to say that colonial censuses did not so much count the peoples of the empire as invent them. The enumerator arrived with a schedule of questions and a list of permitted answers, and the population that emerged from the tables, divided into neatly bounded communities of religion, caste, tribe and language, was in this view largely a creation of the administrative imagination. The argument has done valuable work. It has made historians suspicious of treating census categories as simple descriptions of a pre-existing social order, and it has drawn attention to the way in which knowledge and rule were bound together. Yet in its strongest form it claims too much, and in claiming too much it obscures what was genuinely new.

The distinctions that census officials recorded were not conjured out of nothing. People in the societies being counted had long spoken of lineage, occupation, locality, sect and status, and had used these terms to arrange marriages, settle disputes and decide whom to eat with. What these older vocabularies did not usually require was that a person belong to exactly one category of each kind, at all times and for all purposes. A weaver might describe himself by his craft in the market, by his lineage at a wedding and by his village when travelling, without feeling that any one of these was his real identity. The census demanded a single answer, written once and then aggregated, and it treated a blank or a double answer as an error to be corrected.

The novelty, then, lay less in the categories than in what was done with them. Once fixed and counted, a category could be ranked, compared across districts, and used as the basis for decisions about recruitment, land and, later, political representation. Groups discovered that their recorded size and standing had consequences, and petitions asking to be listed under a different name or placed higher in an official order of precedence became a regular feature of census years. Some observers have treated such petitions as proof that the categories were artificial, mere labels that people manipulated for advantage. But the petitions suggest the opposite conclusion. People do not campaign over labels that mean nothing to them; they campaign over labels that have begun to govern their lives.

This is why the categories outlived the governments that produced them. An identity that is only imposed can be thrown off when its author departs. An identity that has been taken up, argued over and attached to rights and resources becomes part of the social world it was once supposed merely to describe. The census, in short, was neither a mirror nor a fiction. It was closer to a mould, into which existing and quite fluid materials were pressed until they set.

CAT-VARC-P04-010-Q1 · Meaning in context · EasyMCQ
In the final paragraph, the author calls the census 'closer to a mould'. This most nearly means that the census was
  1. (A)an invention that brought into being communities which had never existed before the enumerators arrived.
  2. (B)a faithful copy that reproduced the existing social order exactly as it was, without altering it in any way.
  3. (C)an instrument that gave a fixed shape to social distinctions which already existed but had been fluid.
  4. (D)a slow source of decay that gradually weakened the older identities until they disappeared from social life.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

The sentence explains the image: 'existing and quite fluid materials were pressed until they set'. A mould does not create its material or merely reflect it; it fixes its shape, which matches the argument of paragraphs 2 and 3.

Why not (A): That is the 'fiction' the author also rejects; paragraph 2 says the distinctions 'were not conjured out of nothing'.

Why not (B): That is the 'mirror' the same paragraph rejects; paragraph 2 shows the census changed distinctions by demanding a single answer.

Why not (D): This plays on another sense of the word; the passage says the categories 'outlived the governments', the reverse of decay.

Common misconception: Choosing the 'mirror' or 'fiction' reading, both of which the author explicitly sets aside.

Difficulty: The image is glossed in the same sentence. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-011 · Empire and its aftermath · passage Medium · 495 words · 4 questions

In the decades after the British Empire abolished slavery in the 1830s, sugar planters in colonies such as Mauritius, Trinidad and British Guiana, and later Fiji, turned to a different source of labour: indentured workers, most of them recruited in India, who signed contracts binding them to an estate for a fixed term of years. Ever since, the system has been argued over in terms set by the institution it replaced. Its critics have called it slavery under a new name; its defenders, at the time and since, have insisted that it was a form of voluntary migration, entered into by contract and ended by the contract's expiry. Each side treats the question as one of classification, as though the decisive task were to place indenture on the correct side of the line between slave and free.

There are good reasons to resist the first classification. Indentured workers were not property. They could not be sold, their children did not inherit their status, and at the end of the term many returned home, while others stayed, bought land and built communities whose descendants remain in those countries today. To call this slavery is to lose sight of what was specifically terrible about slavery, and it risks erasing the choices, however constrained, that migrants made. But the second classification fares no better. Recruits were often misled about the work and the wages that awaited them; many were illiterate and signed documents they could not read; and once on the estate they found that the contract, far from protecting them, was the instrument of their confinement.

This last point is the one that both classifications miss. Colonial labour laws typically made it a criminal offence, not merely a breach of contract, for an indentured worker to be absent from work, to leave the estate without a pass, or to refuse a task. A worker who walked away could be arrested, fined or imprisoned, and the time spent in prison might be added to the term he owed. The coercion, in other words, did not stand outside the contract and contradict it; it was written into the contract and enforced by courts. The language of free agreement and the reality of compulsion were not opposed but fused.

Seen this way, indenture is less a halfway house between slavery and freedom than a demonstration that the line between them was never as clean as abolitionists hoped. The abolition of property in persons did not by itself abolish compulsion; it moved compulsion into the law of contract, where it could be presented as the free choice of the person compelled. That is a lesson with uses beyond the nineteenth century. Wherever a worker's right to leave a job is restricted by debt, by the confiscation of documents or by a visa tied to a single employer, the question worth asking is not whether a contract exists, but what the contract permits others to do to the person who signed it.

  3. (C)They were paid wages equal to those earned by free workers in the same colonies.
  4. (D)They were protected by the colonial courts from criminal penalties for absence from work.
Answer and explanation

Paragraph 2 states that indentured workers 'were not property. They could not be sold, their children did not inherit their status'.

Common misconception: Assuming that because indenture was not slavery its workers enjoyed the freedoms of free labour.

CAT-VARC-P04-011-Q3 · Application / analogy · MediumMCQ
Which of the following present-day arrangements would the author regard as most clearly raising the question posed at the end of the passage?
  1. (A)A volunteer who works unpaid for a charity on weekends without having signed any written agreement at all.
  2. (B)A farm worker whose employer lent the recruitment fee and who may not change jobs until the loan, deducted from wages, is repaid.
  3. (C)A freelance designer who loses a client after missing a deadline that was set out in the signed contract.
  4. (D)A worker who is paid less than the contract promised but remains free to resign and look for other work at any time.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

The final paragraph names arrangements in which 'a worker's right to leave a job is restricted by debt, by the confiscation of documents or by a visa tied to a single employer'. A worker tied to the job by an employer's loan is restricted by debt, which is the closest parallel.

Why not (A): With no contract and no restriction on leaving, nothing here resembles compulsion presented as free choice.

Why not (C): Losing a client is an ordinary consequence of breach; the designer's freedom to stop working is not restricted at all.

Why not (D): The author's question concerns restrictions on the right to leave; this worker can leave, so the case is unfair pay, not compulsion through contract.

Common misconception: Choosing a case of unfairness that does not involve restriction on leaving.

Difficulty: Requires applying the precise criterion (restriction on leaving through the contract) to new cases. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-012 · Empire and its aftermath · passage Easy · 438 words · 4 questions

When a colony becomes independent, one of the first questions its new government must answer is which language it will govern in. The question is often posed as a choice between authenticity and convenience. On one side stands the language of the former rulers, already established in the courts, the universities and the upper ranks of the civil service, and useful for dealing with the wider world. On the other stand the languages the people actually speak, whose adoption would seem to complete the work of liberation. Put this way, retaining the colonial language looks like a practical compromise with a humiliating past, and abandoning it looks like a costly act of pride.

This framing, however, hides the most important feature of the decision, which is that every option distributes advantage and disadvantage among citizens. In a country where many languages are spoken and none is spoken by a clear majority, choosing one of them as the sole language of administration hands a lasting head start to those who grew up speaking it. Their children will sit examinations in their mother tongue while everyone else's children sit them in a second language. Groups that expect to lose from such an arrangement can be relied upon to resist it, sometimes fiercely, and their resistance is not mere sentiment but a reasonable reading of their interests.

The colonial language has a curious property in this situation: it is equally foreign to almost everyone. Retaining it therefore spares the state from having to declare one community's speech superior to another's, which is one reason several multilingual states have kept it long after they expected to discard it. But equal foreignness is not equal access. The colonial language is already the property of a small educated class, often urban and often wealthy, and keeping it as the language of power entrenches that class's position against the rural majority of every linguistic group. The choice, then, is not between authenticity and convenience but between two kinds of inequality: one that runs between language communities, and one that runs between classes within each of them.

Recognising this does not settle what any particular country should do, and it is not an argument for pretending the dilemma away. It does suggest that the sharpest debates about language after empire have rarely been about nostalgia or national pride, whatever their rhetoric. They have been about who will be able to read the forms, pass the examinations and understand the judge. Policies that use several languages at different levels of government, though cumbersome, are best understood as attempts to spread these costs rather than to escape them.

Common misconception: Confusing the colonial language's prestige with wide use among the population.

Difficulty: Stated in one sentence of paragraph 3. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-013 · Empire and its aftermath · passage Medium · 420 words · 4 questions

Few images of empire are as familiar as that of European diplomats bent over a map of Africa, ruler in hand, dividing a continent they had barely visited. The image is usually summoned to explain why so many of the continent's borders run in straight lines, and why so many of its states have struggled: the borders, it is said, were artificial, cutting across peoples and lumping together others who had nothing in common, and the conflicts of the post-colonial era are their bitter harvest. The story has the merit of placing responsibility where much of it belongs. As an explanation, however, it is weaker than its popularity suggests.

Begin with the image itself. The conference held in Berlin in 1884 and 1885 is often remembered as the moment of partition, but it chiefly agreed on rules by which European powers would recognise one another's claims. Most actual boundaries were settled afterwards, over decades, through bilateral treaties, exchanges of territory and survey commissions, and many of them followed rivers, watersheds or the limits of existing polities rather than lines of latitude. The straight lines are real, but they cluster in deserts and thinly populated regions, where they divided relatively few people.

More fundamentally, the charge of artificiality proves too much. Every border is artificial in the sense that people drew it, and many of the borders of Europe were fixed by wars, dynastic marriages and peace conferences no more attentive to local wishes than any colonial commission. If artificial borders caused instability by themselves, much of the world would be permanently at war. Nor is it obvious what a natural border would be. Peoples overlap, migrate and intermarry; a line that united one group would divide another. When African states gained independence, their leaders, aware of these difficulties, chose through the Organisation of African Unity to accept the boundaries they had inherited rather than reopen them, judging that an attempt to redraw them would be more dangerous than living with them.

None of this acquits the colonial powers. Boundaries were indeed drawn without consulting the people they affected, and some did separate communities from their pastures, markets and kin. But the more serious legacy lay inside the lines rather than in their placement: states built for extraction, with administrations designed to collect revenue and keep order rather than to represent, and with ethnic categories hardened by colonial policy into the main currency of politics. To blame the shape of the map is to look at the frame and miss the picture.

  3. (C)were drawn at the Berlin conference by diplomats who settled each boundary with a ruler on the map.
  4. (D)have caused most of the wars fought between African states in the decades since independence was gained.

Paragraph 2 states: 'The straight lines are real, but they cluster in deserts and thinly populated regions, where they divided relatively few people.'

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 says the conference 'chiefly agreed on rules' and that most boundaries 'were settled afterwards, over decades'.

Why not (D): This is the popular explanation that the author says 'is weaker than its popularity suggests'; the passage never attributes most wars to them.

Common misconception: Accepting the familiar image of partition that paragraph 2 corrects.

Difficulty: Located in one sentence of paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-014 · Empire and its aftermath · passage Medium · 432 words · 4 questions

Indirect rule was presented by its architects as an act of restraint. Rather than replace the institutions of the peoples it governed, the colonial state would work through them: chiefs would keep their offices, customary courts would apply customary law, and the colonial officer would advise rather than command. The policy, most closely associated with British administration in Nigeria and later extended across much of British Africa, was cheaper than direct administration, and its defenders also claimed for it a moral advantage. It would let colonised peoples develop along their own lines instead of being turned into imitation Europeans.

The difficulty was that the institutions indirect rule claimed to preserve rarely existed in the form it required. The policy needed a chief: one identifiable person in each locality who could collect tax, supply labour and answer for order. Where centralised kingdoms existed, such a figure could be found. Where authority had been spread among councils of elders, age-grades and religious associations, as in parts of south-eastern Nigeria, officials appointed men to the role, and the so-called warrant chiefs they created had little standing in local tradition. Even where genuine rulers existed, their position changed. A king who had once had to keep the support of title-holders and rivals, and who faced the threat that subjects might desert him, now had the backing of a colonial army, and the checks that had limited him lost much of their force.

Customary law underwent a similar transformation. To be applied in courts, custom had to be written down, and the people asked to state it were usually senior men with an interest in the answer. Rules about land, marriage and inheritance that had been flexible and contested were recorded as fixed, and frequently in the form most favourable to elders against juniors and men against women. Custom that had changed with circumstances became a code that could be changed only by the administration.

It would be wrong, however, to conclude that tradition was simply invented by colonial officials and imposed on passive subjects. The elders who told officers what the custom was were pursuing their own ends, and they often succeeded; the codes bear their fingerprints as much as the administrators'. That is precisely what makes indirect rule instructive. Its most lasting effect was not the preservation of old authority, nor its outright replacement, but the creation of a new kind of authority that spoke the language of tradition while answering upward to the state rather than downward to the governed. Many post-colonial governments, finding such authority convenient, kept it.

Common misconception: Accepting the policy's own description of itself as preservation.

CAT-VARC-P04-015 · Empire and its aftermath · passage Difficult · 582 words · 5 questions

The doctrine that a territory could be treated as belonging to no one, terra nullius, is often described as a lie that settlers told themselves. The lands of Australia or North America, the argument runs, were plainly inhabited; to declare them empty was either a culpable ignorance or a convenient fiction, and once the facts are acknowledged the doctrine collapses. There is something right in this, but it misdescribes the doctrine's real strength. Most of the people who invoked the idea of empty land were perfectly aware that others lived there. What they denied was not presence but a particular kind of relationship to the land, one that their own law was prepared to recognise as ownership.

The intellectual resources for that denial were readily available. An influential strand of early modern European thought held that property arose when a person mixed his labour with the earth, enclosing it, ploughing it and improving it; John Locke's well-known version of the argument drew explicitly on the example of America. On this view, land that was hunted over, gathered from, burned to renew its grasses or moved across with the seasons had not yet been appropriated, however long people had lived on it. The settlers' error, if it was one, was not a failure to see but a theory of what was worth seeing. Fences, fields and permanent buildings registered as use; songs that mapped water sources, rules governing who might hunt where, and the careful management of fire did not.

This matters because the doctrine has now been formally repudiated in several places, most famously when the High Court of Australia in 1992 rejected the proposition that the continent had belonged to no one at the time of British settlement, a proposition on which much of the country's land law had quietly rested. The repudiation was a real achievement, and it would be ungracious to belittle it. Yet the regimes of native title that followed typically require claimants to show that they have maintained a continuing connection with the land under traditional laws and customs, often to the satisfaction of courts that weigh written records and documented occupation heavily. Communities that were driven off their country, or whose practices were suppressed, find that the very dispossession they complain of becomes evidence against them: a broken connection, however it was broken, is a weaker claim.

In other words, the factual premise of terra nullius has been abandoned while something like its theoretical premise survives. Title still depends on displaying a relationship to land in forms that a court is equipped to recognise, and the forms most easily recognised are those that leave records. The burden has simply been reversed. Where the settler state once declared the land empty and left the inhabitants to prove otherwise in practice, the modern claimant must now prove, in a forum designed by the settler state, that the land was never emptied of them.

It does not follow that recognition is worthless, or that some purer form of restitution is readily available. Any legal system must have rules of proof, and claims that could be made without evidence would invite abuse. The point is narrower. A doctrine is not fully dismantled when its conclusion is reversed if the standard used to apply the reversal was shaped by the doctrine itself. To finish the work would require asking not only whether people were present, which no one now denies, but whose ways of being present count as having a place.

  4. (D)Hunting over the same territory for many generations.

Paragraph 2 says property arose when a person mixed labour with the earth, 'enclosing it, ploughing it and improving it', and that 'Fences, fields and permanent buildings registered as use'.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 lists land 'burned to renew its grasses' among land that 'had not yet been appropriated'.

Why not (D): Land 'hunted over' is listed as not yet appropriated, 'however long people had lived on it'.

Common misconception: Assuming that long or careful use of land must have counted as ownership.

Difficulty: Stated directly in paragraph 2; each wrong option is listed there as not counting. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-015-Q2 · Inference · MediumMCQ
The remark that the settlers' error was 'not a failure to see but a theory of what was worth seeing' (paragraph 2) implies that
  1. (A)the land practices of the original inhabitants were in fact less productive than European agriculture.
  2. (B)the settlers were unaware of the methods by which the original inhabitants managed and used the land.
  3. (C)the doctrine could not be overturned merely by establishing that the land had been inhabited.
  4. (D)the settlers knowingly told a lie in order to justify taking land they knew belonged to others.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

If the error lay in a theory about which relationships count as ownership rather than in a failure of observation, then showing that people were present does not touch it. Paragraph 1 says the familiar argument assumes the doctrine 'collapses' once the facts are acknowledged, which the author disputes.

Why not (A): The passage makes no comparison of productivity; it describes which practices a theory chose to recognise as use.

Why not (B): Paragraph 1 says those who invoked the idea 'were perfectly aware that others lived there'; the error was not ignorance.

Why not (D): The author says the doctrine is wrongly 'described as a lie'; settlers denied ownership by their own theory, not knowingly falsely.

Common misconception: Reading 'error' as ignorance or as deliberate deceit, both of which the author sets aside.

Difficulty: Requires connecting the remark in paragraph 2 to the argument the author rejects in paragraph 1. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET
