CAT-VARC-P04-016 · Empire and its aftermath · passage Difficult · 637 words · 5 questions

The end of empire is usually pictured as a ceremony. A flag is lowered at midnight, another is raised, a governor boards a ship, and a new state takes its seat in the international order. Historians have long known that the picture is too tidy, and a powerful critique, voiced by some of the leaders of newly independent states themselves and given the name neo-colonialism, holds that it is not merely tidy but false. On this account, formal independence changed the personnel and the symbols of rule while leaving its substance intact: the new states still sold raw materials at prices set elsewhere, borrowed from foreign banks, bought foreign manufactures and relied on foreign advisers. The flag changed; the economy did not.

The critique captured something the ceremonial picture ignores, and it remains a useful corrective to triumphal accounts. Yet it shares more with the view it attacks than either side usually admits. Both treat empire as a single thing that either ends or persists, so that the only question is whether the moment of independence marked its end. Empire was not a single thing. It was a bundle of relations, held together by the power of the imperial state: sovereignty over territory and law, control of trade and currency, the organisation of armed force, the training of an administrative and professional class, the language in which public life was conducted, and the habits of deference and aspiration that all these produced. There is no reason to expect such strands to have come apart at the same speed, and they did not.

Sovereignty, the strand that the ceremony marked, ended at a stroke, and its ending was not trivial. A sovereign government could nationalise a foreign-owned mine, change its alliances, write a new constitution, expel a foreign base or make ruinous mistakes of its own, and many did each of these things. Other strands loosened over decades. Monetary arrangements tying several former colonies' currencies to that of the former ruler persisted long after independence; legal systems, curricula and civil-service procedures inherited from the colonial period often outlasted the governments that had installed them; the former metropolis remained, for many, the place where one went to study and to be taken seriously. Some of these continuities were imposed through unequal bargaining at the moment of transfer. Others were chosen, and defended, by new elites who had been formed by the old system and found its institutions congenial.

Seen as an unbundling, decolonisation ceases to be an event that either happened or did not, and becomes a set of processes whose timing differed by strand and by place. This has a practical as well as a historiographical consequence. The question 'When did empire end?' admits no good answer, and debates conducted in its terms tend to stall, with one side pointing to the ceremonies and the other to the trade figures. The better question is which relations were transformed, when, by whom and at what cost. It allows one to say, without contradiction, that a country became fully sovereign in a given year and that its banking system or its universities remained oriented toward its former ruler for a generation.

It also places responsibility more precisely. If the persistence of colonial patterns were simply the continuation of empire by other means, then the governments of independent states would be its passive victims. If some strands were retained by choice, those governments must answer for the choosing, just as former imperial powers must answer for the strands they kept by pressure. The unbundled view is less satisfying than either of the stories it replaces, since it yields no single date and no single villain. That is not a weakness in a historical account; it is usually a sign that the account has begun to fit its subject.

  2. (B)a power that formal independence genuinely conferred, whatever the wisdom of exercising it in a given case.
  3. (C)one of the ruinous mistakes that showed many new governments were not yet ready for sovereignty.
  4. (D)a concession that former imperial powers demanded of new states during the bargaining over the transfer of power.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 3 says sovereignty's ending 'was not trivial' and lists what 'A sovereign government could' do, beginning with nationalising a foreign-owned mine. The example shows a real power gained at independence, and the inclusion of 'ruinous mistakes' shows the author is not judging whether each use was wise.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 says other strands, including monetary arrangements, 'loosened over decades', so dependence did not end with sovereignty.

Why not (C): Mistakes are listed as a separate item in the same sentence; the author does not call nationalisation a mistake or judge readiness.

Why not (D): Nationalising a foreign-owned mine works against foreign interests; the passage lists it among powers of a 'sovereign government'.

Common misconception: Treating every item in the list as a mistake, or confusing sovereignty with economic independence.

Difficulty: The example's role follows from the claim it illustrates. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-017 · State, law and citizenship · passage Easy · 429 words · 4 questions

Before the uniformed police forces that appeared in many large cities during the nineteenth century, the keeping of order was a patchwork. Night watchmen walked fixed rounds, parish constables served unpaid and often unwillingly, and private thief-takers recovered stolen goods for a fee, sometimes from the very thieves they claimed to pursue. What was new about the reformed police was not that they caught criminals more efficiently. It was the claim that a salaried, disciplined body of officers, visible on the street at all hours, could prevent offences from occurring at all. The patrol, rather than the arrest, was meant to be the core of the institution.

This founding idea has an awkward property: its successes cannot be seen. A burglary that does not happen leaves no record, and a street that stays quiet because an officer passed through it twenty minutes earlier looks exactly like a street that would have stayed quiet anyway. The arrest, by contrast, is countable. It produces a name, a charge and a figure that can be reported upward and compared from one year to the next. Over time, institutions tend to drift toward what they can count, and police forces have been no exception. Officers come to be judged by how many people they detain, and the quiet beat, which was supposed to be the whole point, comes to look like idleness.

It would be too simple to conclude that the preventive ideal was therefore an illusion. Critics sometimes point out that putting more officers on the street does not reliably reduce recorded crime, and they are right to be sceptical of large claims about deterrence. But the patrol was never only about deterrence. A visible and predictable presence tells residents that someone is responsible for the shared space of the street, and that sense of being looked after shapes whether people walk out at night, whether they report what they see, and whether they regard the police as theirs or as an occupying force. These effects are as hard to count as the burglary that did not happen, but they are not imaginary.

The deeper lesson concerns what happens when a public institution is measured by its most legible output rather than by its purpose. A force that rewards arrests will produce arrests, and it may do so in ways that erode the trust on which prevention depends. Recovering the original idea does not mean abandoning measurement; it means admitting that some of the most important things a police force does will show up, if at all, only as an absence.

  3. (C)the uniformed police replaced watchmen and constables chiefly because they caught offenders far more efficiently than the older arrangements had.

Why not (C): Paragraph 1 says the opposite: 'What was new about the reformed police was not that they caught criminals more efficiently.'

Common misconception: Taking the critics' view in paragraph 3 for the author's, or inflating the author's case into a call to stop measuring.

Difficulty: One wrong option is the reported view the author rejects and another overstates the conclusion; the correct answer needs the whole arc. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-018 · State, law and citizenship · passage Easy · 425 words · 4 questions

We tend to think of the passport as a document that lets people travel. It is the thing we pack and check twice before leaving for the airport, and losing it abroad feels like being stranded. Yet the history of the passport suggests that its first purpose was not to open borders but to give states a means of closing them selectively. A document that certifies who you are, and which state answers for you, is above all a tool for deciding who may enter, who may leave, and who may be turned back.

For much of the nineteenth century, a traveller could cross many European frontiers without being asked for papers at all. Controls existed, but they were patchy, and in several countries they were relaxed as railways made movement faster and cheaper. The First World War changed this. Governments anxious about spies, deserters and the flight of men of fighting age began demanding documents at every crossing, and when the war ended the requirement was not withdrawn. What had been introduced as an emergency measure became the ordinary condition of international travel, and the expectation that every person should belong to exactly one state, and carry proof of it, hardened into common sense. Few people now remember that it was ever otherwise.

None of this makes the passport simply an instrument of control. For the person who holds it, it is also a claim on protection: it entitles the bearer to return home and to seek help from the officials of the issuing state when abroad. The predicament of stateless people, who have no government willing to vouch for them, shows how much that claim is worth. Without it, a person can be refused entry everywhere and expelled from wherever he happens to be.

The more uncomfortable point is that the protection is unevenly distributed. Two travellers standing in the same queue may carry documents of identical design and very different power, one admitting its holder to most of the world without a visa, the other inviting suspicion at every desk. What the passport records, then, is not only identity but rank in an order of states, an accident of birth that the individual did little to earn and can rarely change. Nor is that rank settled by anything the traveller does at the desk; it is negotiated between governments, through visa agreements and reciprocal arrangements, long before any individual reaches the frontier. To call the document a passport to freedom is to describe the experience of those whose papers happen to work.

  3. (C)was withdrawn once fears about spies had passed and was reintroduced only much later.
  4. (D)was resisted by most travellers, who regarded it as an unjustified intrusion on their liberty.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 2 says documents were demanded because of wartime fears about 'spies, deserters' and fleeing men, and that 'when the war ended the requirement was not withdrawn', so the measure outlasted its original reasons.

Why not (D): Travellers' reactions are never described; the passage says the expectation 'hardened into common sense'.

Common misconception: Misreading the railway sentence as a cause of controls rather than of their relaxation.

Difficulty: The inference follows from two adjacent sentences. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 says the passport entitles the bearer 'to seek help from the officials of the issuing state when abroad'.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 says the passport records 'rank in an order of states', an accident of birth the individual 'can rarely change'.

Common misconception: Remembering that railways are mentioned and assuming they explain the requirement.

Difficulty: Three options must be checked against three different paragraphs. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-019 · State, law and citizenship · passage Medium · 444 words · 4 questions

Public debate about bureaucracy tends to oscillate between two complaints. One is that officials are rigid, applying rules mechanically to people whose circumstances the rules never anticipated. The other is that officials are arbitrary, treating similar applicants differently according to mood, prejudice or the length of the queue. The usual remedy for the second complaint is more rules: tighter eligibility criteria, standard forms, checklists that leave nothing to judgement. Each new scandal of favouritism produces another layer of instructions, and the layers accumulate. The remedy is seldom questioned, because it seems obvious that a rule applied uniformly is fairer than a person deciding as he pleases.

What this remedy overlooks is that the officials who deal directly with the public, the benefits clerk, the housing inspector, the teacher deciding which pupil needs extra help, cannot stop exercising judgement however detailed their instructions become. A rule says that an applicant must provide proof of address; it does not say how hard the clerk should look for an acceptable document when the applicant has been living with relatives. Moreover, such officials almost always have more cases than time. They must decide whose file to open first, whose story to probe and whose to accept, and these decisions shape outcomes as surely as any formal ruling. When rules are tightened, judgement does not disappear. It retreats into these less visible choices, where it is harder to observe and correct.

This is not to romanticise frontline discretion. The same latitude that allows a sympathetic clerk to find a way through for a desperate family allows an unsympathetic one to find reasons for delay, and there is ample reason to think that such latitude is not exercised evenly across applicants of different backgrounds. The case for discretion is not that officials can be trusted. It is that the choice is not between discretion and its absence, but between discretion that is acknowledged and discretion that is driven underground. Discretion of the first kind can be examined and improved; discretion of the second kind can only be suspected.

Seen this way, the task of administrative reform changes. Instead of writing rules ever more finely in the hope of closing every gap, an agency might record the informal decisions its staff actually make, review samples of cases to see how similar applicants fare, and give officials explicit guidance on the purposes their judgement should serve. None of these steps would remove judgement from the counter; they would make it something an agency can see, discuss and, where necessary, correct. Such measures concede what the rule-writer prefers to deny: that every system of rules ends, at its edges, in someone deciding.

CAT-VARC-P04-019-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · MediumMCQ

  2. (B)Most of the unfairness in public agencies arises because officials are given more cases than they can handle in the time available.
  3. (C)Frontline officials should be given wide freedom to depart from rules, because they understand applicants' circumstances better than those who write the rules.
  4. (D)Detailed rules remain the fairest way to treat applicants alike, although officials sometimes apply such rules more rigidly than they should.
Answer and explanation

Paragraph 2 argues that judgement cannot be eliminated, paragraph 3 that the real choice is between 'discretion that is acknowledged and discretion that is driven underground', and paragraph 4 proposes recording, reviewing and guiding it.

Why not (B): Heavy caseloads appear in paragraph 2 as one reason judgement is unavoidable; the passage does not make them the main source of unfairness.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 says 'The case for discretion is not that officials can be trusted', and warns that latitude can be used to delay as well as to help.

Why not (D): The passage questions the remedy of more rules, arguing in paragraph 2 that tightened rules only drive judgement into 'less visible choices'.

Common misconception: Reading the defence of discretion as trust in officials, which paragraph 3 explicitly disowns.

Difficulty: The tempting wrong option praises discretion, which the author accepts only in a qualified way. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-020 · State, law and citizenship · passage Medium · 438 words · 4 questions

Discussions of nationality law often begin from a tidy contrast. Under the principle of soil, a child born within the territory of a state becomes its citizen; under the principle of descent, citizenship passes from parent to child wherever the child is born. The first is commonly described as civic and open, since it admits anyone born on the land regardless of ancestry; the second as ethnic and closed, since it ties membership to bloodline. The contrast is useful as a first approximation, but as a guide to which societies are more welcoming it is close to misleading.

Consider why states adopted the soil rule in the first place. Where governments needed to populate large territories and to secure soldiers and taxpayers, it was convenient to claim the children of newcomers automatically, whatever their parents' wishes or origins. The rule expressed less a generous conception of belonging than an interest in numbers. Conversely, the descent rule has served countries with large populations abroad, allowing the children of emigrants to retain a connection to a homeland they may visit only occasionally. Neither motive is obviously more liberal than the other, and the same rule can serve quite different ends depending on who adopts it and why.

More importantly, the rule that governs birth is only one part of a citizenship regime, and often not the part that matters most. A state that follows the descent principle but allows long-term residents to naturalise after a few years, and extends citizenship to children who have grown up and been schooled in the country, may be far easier to join than a state that follows the soil principle but makes naturalisation slow, costly and uncertain for adults. The second state includes every child born on its territory and keeps their parents waiting at the door indefinitely. A single household may then contain citizens and foreigners under one roof, divided by nothing more than the place where each member happened to be born.

None of this means the choice of birth rule is unimportant. The soil principle does guarantee something valuable: no one born in the territory can be left, by the mere fact of birth, a permanent outsider in the only country he has known, and states without it have sometimes produced generations of residents with no secure status. But that guarantee is better understood as one protection among several than as the defining mark of an open society. The question worth asking of any nationality law is not which principle it starts from, but how readily it turns people who have made their lives in a country into its members.

CAT-VARC-P04-020-Q1 · Inference · MediumMCQ

  2. (B)was abandoned by most states once their need for soldiers and taxpayers had declined over time.
  3. (C)was imposed on immigrant parents against their wishes and was widely resented by the families it affected.
  4. (D)is in practice more exclusive than the descent principle, despite its reputation for being open.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 2 says the soil rule 'expressed less a generous conception of belonging than an interest in numbers', tied to the need for 'soldiers and taxpayers'.

Why not (B): The passage gives no account of the soil rule being abandoned; it discusses only why it was adopted.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 says the children were claimed regardless of what their parents wanted, which means the parents were not consulted; it says nothing about resentment.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 concludes that 'Neither motive is obviously more liberal than the other', which is weaker than reversing the ranking.

Common misconception: Overshooting the author's levelling claim into a reversal of the usual ranking.

Difficulty: The tempting wrong option overstates 'neither ... more liberal' into 'more exclusive'. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-021 · State, law and citizenship · passage Medium · 447 words · 4 questions

There is an old argument, revived by students of public finance, that the way a state raises its money shapes the kind of state it becomes. A government that depends on taxing its own population must, to some degree, negotiate with that population. It needs to know who earns what and where they live; it needs officials who can assess, collect and keep accounts; and it needs taxpayers who will comply without being coerced at every turn. Each of these needs creates leverage for the governed. Those who pay can demand to know how their money is spent, and rulers who want a steady revenue have reason to listen.

A government that draws most of its income from a single lucrative resource, such as oil or minerals sold abroad, faces no comparable pressure. Its revenue arrives through a handful of contracts with foreign buyers and requires little cooperation from ordinary citizens. It has no need to build an apparatus that knows the population in detail, and no need to trade services or a voice in decisions for compliance. Proponents of the argument suggest that such states can afford to be both unresponsive and administratively thin, buying acquiescence with subsidies and public employment rather than earning consent through bargaining.

Stated baldly, the thesis is too mechanical. Taxation has often been extracted by force rather than negotiated, and many heavily taxed populations have obtained neither representation nor accountability in return. Nor are all resource-rich states alike: where competent institutions and habits of public scrutiny were established before a windfall arrived, the revenue has sometimes been managed with considerable openness. The sequence appears to matter as much as the source. A resource boom that lands on a weak state may keep it weak; one that lands on a state already accustomed to accounting for itself need not.

What survives these qualifications is a narrower but sturdier claim. The point is not that taxes make governments good or that resource revenue makes them bad, but that accountability tends to grow where rulers need something from the ruled that cannot simply be seized. Taxation is one common source of that need, perhaps historically the most important, but it is the dependence, not the tax form as such, that does the work. A reform that raised more revenue from citizens without increasing what citizens could demand in return would therefore miss the point entirely, and might even entrench the habits of command it was meant to cure. Equally, a resource-rich government that chose to make itself dependent on its citizens, by funding services through broad taxes and setting its windfall aside, would be doing something more significant than balancing its books.

  4. (D)a defence of the thesis that resource revenue inevitably makes governments unaccountable, against critics who doubt that it does.

Answer: (B)

Paragraphs 1 and 2 present the thesis, paragraph 3 qualifies it with two kinds of counter-case, and paragraph 4 keeps 'a narrower but sturdier claim' about dependence.

Why not (A): Such states appear in one sentence of paragraph 3 as a counter-case; no particular states are described and they are not the passage's subject.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 retains 'a narrower but sturdier claim' about dependence, so the author does not reject the link outright.

Why not (D): Paragraph 3 calls the thesis 'too mechanical', and paragraph 4 denies that 'resource revenue makes them bad'; the author qualifies rather than defends it.

Common misconception: Choosing the 'defence' option because the passage spends two paragraphs setting out the thesis sympathetically.

Difficulty: Requires seeing that the author reports, qualifies and then narrows the thesis. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-022 · State, law and citizenship · passage Medium · 456 words · 4 questions

Almost every modern constitution makes room for its own partial suspension. In a war, an epidemic or a sudden collapse of public order, the executive may be permitted to rule by decree, restrict movement or detain people without the usual procedures, on the understanding that these powers serve to preserve the constitutional order rather than to replace it. The logic is respectable. A constitution that made no provision for emergencies would not prevent governments from acting in a crisis; it would simply ensure that they acted outside the law, improvising powers with no defined limits and no defined end.

The danger that emergency provisions are usually designed to guard against is the dramatic one: a leader who declares a crisis in order to abolish the constitution altogether. That danger is real but comparatively rare. The more common pattern is quieter. An emergency is declared for a genuine reason, and then renewed, and renewed again, because the threat has not entirely disappeared and no official wants to be blamed if it returns. Measures adopted as exceptions are gradually written into ordinary statutes, where they no longer need renewal at all. And each emergency lowers the threshold for the next, since officials and citizens alike grow accustomed to powers that once seemed extraordinary.

The standard remedy is the sunset clause, which makes emergency powers lapse after a fixed period unless they are renewed. Sunset clauses are better than nothing, but they tend to turn renewal into a formality, a vote taken under the shadow of the original fear by legislators who see little to gain from being the ones who let the powers lapse. What matters is not whether powers expire but how hard it is to extend them. A more demanding design would require larger majorities for each successive renewal, so that a measure which commanded a bare majority at the outset needed broad agreement to survive a year later, and it would oblige the government to state publicly, each time, why ordinary law is still inadequate to the threat.

Such designs would not prevent a determined government from abusing emergency powers, and no constitutional text can do that alone; courts, a free press and an opposition willing to make itself unpopular all have parts to play. The purpose of the design is more modest: to make normalisation visible and expensive, so that an exception which has outlasted its reasons must be defended openly as a permanent change rather than slipping into permanence unnoticed. A government that genuinely needs lasting powers can still obtain them, but only by persuading a broad majority that the change should be lasting. The test of an emergency regime is less whether it can survive a coup than whether it can end.

CAT-VARC-P04-022-Q1 · Inference · MediumMCQ

  2. (B)officials fear being blamed for lifting powers too early more than they fear keeping them too long.
  3. (C)citizens demand that the powers stay in place long after officials would prefer to lift them.
  4. (D)leaders invent threats in order to dismantle the constitution gradually, one renewal at a time.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 2 says emergencies are renewed 'because the threat has not entirely disappeared and no official wants to be blamed if it returns'. The asymmetry of blame is what keeps the powers alive.

Why not (A): The passage says only that the threat 'has not entirely disappeared', which implies a lingering threat, not a growing one.

Why not (C): The passage gives no evidence of public demand for renewal; it attributes renewal to officials' reluctance to be blamed.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says the common case begins with an emergency 'declared for a genuine reason'; deliberate abolition is the rarer, dramatic danger.

Common misconception: Confusing the quiet pattern with the dramatic danger the paragraph sets aside.

Difficulty: Requires paraphrasing the stated reason and separating it from the dramatic case. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

The author's attitude towards sunset clauses is best described as
  1. (A)qualified approval: they help somewhat but leave renewal far too easy to secure.
  2. (B)strong endorsement: they are the most effective safeguard that a written constitution can provide.
  3. (C)grudging hostility: they are a distraction that has delayed the adoption of better designs.
  4. (D)outright rejection: they make abuse of emergency powers more likely than having no time limit at all.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 3 calls sunset clauses 'better than nothing' but says they 'turn renewal into a formality', and proposes rising majorities instead.

Why not (B): The author proposes 'A more demanding design' because sunset clauses turn renewal into 'a formality'.

Why not (C): The passage never says sunset clauses delayed anything; it says they are better than nothing but insufficient.

Why not (D): Paragraph 3 says 'Sunset clauses are better than nothing', which rules out outright rejection.

Common misconception: Reading the criticism of sunset clauses as total rejection.

Difficulty: The attitude is mixed and the correct answer must match both halves. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-023 · State, law and citizenship · passage Difficult · 584 words · 5 questions

Few political ideals are invoked as often, or defined as loosely, as the rule of law. In one usage it names a set of formal properties of a legal order: laws should be general rather than aimed at particular persons, public rather than secret, prospective rather than retroactive, clear enough to be followed, stable enough to be relied on, and applied by courts that are independent of those who hold power. In another usage the phrase stands for a much larger package. A state governed by the rule of law, on this richer view, is one that also protects fundamental rights, holds free elections and distributes its burdens fairly. Whatever is admirable in a constitutional democracy tends to be gathered under the same heading.

The richer view has an obvious appeal. It seems perverse to praise a legal order for its clarity and consistency while it applies clear and consistent laws that strip a minority of its property or bar it from public life. Yet the inclusive definition carries a cost that its defenders seldom count. If the rule of law means everything good about a political system, the phrase ceases to pick out anything in particular. We lose the ability to say, of one regime, that its laws are cruel but at least public and predictable, or of another that it has excellent statutes on paper which officials ignore whenever it suits them. These are different failings, calling for different remedies, and a vocabulary that cannot separate them is a poorer instrument for judging governments, not a more generous one.

The formal conception, moreover, is not as morally empty as its critics allege. A law that is public and prospective allows people to know in advance what will be required of them and to arrange their lives around it; a law applied by independent courts constrains the officials who administer it as well as the citizens who are subject to it. These are real goods even when the content of the law is objectionable, because they leave the governed some room to anticipate, avoid and contest what is done to them. It is also worth noticing that governments bent on oppression rarely find formal legality congenial. They tend to prefer secret directives, retroactive offences, vague charges that can be stretched to fit any critic, and enforcement that falls on enemies and spares friends. The formal requirements, in other words, get in the way of a great deal of injustice, even if they forbid none of it outright.

This last point should not be pressed too far. The tendency of formality to obstruct oppression is a matter of experience, not of logic, and experience includes regimes that pursued grave injustice through meticulously drafted, publicly promulgated and impartially enforced laws. A defender of the thin conception must concede that legality of this kind is compatible with deep wrong, and that its tendency to discourage abuse is a probability rather than a guarantee. What can be claimed is narrower: that formal legality is one distinct virtue a legal order may have or lack, that its absence makes most other wrongs easier to commit, and that we see it most clearly when we refuse to let it stand for every other virtue at once. Questions about whether the laws are just, whether the rulers were chosen freely and whether the burdens are fairly shared remain, on this view, as urgent as ever; they are simply different questions, and they deserve to be asked in their own terms.

CAT-VARC-P04-023-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · MediumMCQ
The author's primary purpose in the passage is to
  1. (A)defend a formal understanding of the rule of law as naming a real but limited virtue that should be kept apart from other political goods.
  2. (B)compare the formal and richer conceptions of the rule of law and conclude that the choice between them is a merely verbal matter.
  3. (C)show that the formal conception of the rule of law prevents oppressive regimes from enacting and enforcing unjust laws against their subjects.
  4. (D)argue that the rule of law should be defined to include rights and fair distribution, since formal properties on their own are morally empty.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 2 argues against folding every good into the phrase, paragraph 3 defends the moral worth of formality, and paragraph 4 limits that defence to the claim that formal legality is 'one distinct virtue'.

Why not (B): The author holds that the choice matters, because the inclusive vocabulary 'cannot separate' different failings and is 'a poorer instrument'.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 says formal requirements 'forbid none of it outright', and paragraph 4 concedes that formal legality 'is compatible with deep wrong'.

Why not (D): This is the richer view the author argues against in paragraph 2, and paragraph 3 denies that the formal conception is 'morally empty'.

Common misconception: Choosing the 'prevents' option because paragraph 3 praises formality, and missing paragraph 4's concession.

Difficulty: Wrong options catch readers who stop at paragraph 3 or who misread the author's interest in vocabulary as indifference. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P04-024 · State, law and citizenship · passage Difficult · 581 words · 5 questions

Every entrenched constitution invites an awkward question. If a majority of citizens today wishes to change some arrangement, why should it be prevented from doing so by rules that were drafted generations ago by people who are now dead, and who could not have imagined the conditions under which their successors live? A requirement that amendments win two-thirds of the legislature, or the approval of most regions, looks from this angle like a device for letting the past outvote the present. The objection has a democratic edge that is hard to dismiss: it asks by what right one generation sets the terms on which another must govern itself. Nor is it answered by praising the wisdom of the framers, since wisdom about one century is no warrant for authority over the next.

The most familiar answer appeals to self-binding. Just as a sailor who knows he will be tempted by a dangerous song may order his crew to tie him to the mast and ignore his later pleas, a people may bind itself in a calm moment against the passions it expects to feel in an agitated one. Entrenchment, on this account, is not the past overruling the present but the present protecting itself from its own worst impulses. The image is attractive, but it does not fit the case. The sailor binds himself, and the man who later strains at the ropes is the same man who gave the order. A constitution binds people who were not present when it was adopted and who gave no order at all. To call this self-binding is to assume precisely the continuity of the collective self that the objection calls into question.

A better defence starts from a different picture of what entrenchment does. Amendment rules do not, in practice, pit the living against the dead, because the dead cast no votes. What such rules do is set the terms on which living citizens must settle disagreements among themselves. When an amendment fails for want of a supermajority, it is defeated not by its framers but by a present minority large enough to block it. The requirement thus protects groups that might otherwise be outvoted on matters basic to their standing, and it encourages proposals that can attract support beyond a single faction. The protection is not merely theoretical: a group that knows it can block changes to its basic standing has less reason to fear the ordinary swings of electoral fortune, and more reason to accept defeat on everything else. Seen this way, the hand that restrains the majority is a living hand, and the real question is whether present minorities deserve such a veto, not whether the past deserves obedience.

This reframing does not make the original worry vanish. It succeeds only where amendment is demanding but genuinely possible. A constitution whose amendment procedure is so onerous that no change has been achieved in living memory, or which declares certain provisions unamendable altogether, really does impose the judgement of its authors on everyone who comes after; there, no present coalition, however broad, can alter the text. The objection from the dead hand is therefore best understood not as a refutation of entrenchment as such but as a warning about its extremes. Between a constitution that any passing majority may rewrite and one that no one can, the useful argument concerns how much agreement change should require, and that is a question each generation is entitled to keep asking.

  3. (C)explain why amendment rules often require the approval of most regions as well as of the legislature.
  4. (D)suggest that constitutions should be easier to amend in calm periods than in times of political agitation.

Why not (C): Regional approval is mentioned in paragraph 1 as an example of entrenchment; the sailor does not explain it.

Why not (D): The passage makes no proposal about timing amendments; calm and agitation belong to the image of self-binding only.

Common misconception: Taking the view the example illustrates for the view the author holds.

Difficulty: The function is signalled by 'Just as' and the rejection that follows. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-025 · Democracy and participation · passage Medium · 470 words · 4 questions

Elections are so closely identified with democracy that it can seem perverse to ask whether they are democratic at all. Yet consider what an election rewards. To win, a candidate must stand out from the crowd: be better known, better funded, better connected or simply more persuasive on a platform than the people whose votes are sought. An election is, in this sense, a procedure for picking a distinguished few, and the chambers it fills tend to resemble the electorate less closely than a random sample of that electorate would. Many ancient Greek writers saw the point clearly; for them, filling offices by lot was the characteristically democratic method, and election was a device that favoured the prominent.

The modern case for selection by lot builds on that old intuition. A body drawn at random from the adult population brings into the room the people who are usually spoken about rather than heard: the shift worker, the pensioner, the young parent who would never run for anything. Its members owe nothing to donors or to party managers, since nobody campaigned to put them there. Above all, they are free to change their minds. An elected representative who revises a position after hearing the evidence risks being called a turncoat; a citizen chosen by lot has no platform to betray, and so can treat a question as something to be worked out rather than a position to be defended.

Critics usually reply that ordinary citizens lack the competence to legislate. This objection is weaker than it sounds. Few elected legislators are experts in more than a sliver of what they vote on; they rely on staff, committees and witnesses, and there is no reason a randomly chosen body could not do the same. A more serious difficulty concerns accountability. An elected member who disappoints can be removed at the next election, and the prospect of removal disciplines conduct in between. A member chosen by lot answers to no one, and the very independence that shields such a member from donors also shields him or her from voters. Those who propose replacing elected chambers with lotteries have not, to my mind, answered this objection.

The sensible conclusion is not that lot should displace election but that it can do something election does badly. On some questions elected politicians have every incentive to avoid committing themselves, because any clear answer will cost them votes. A panel chosen at random, given time, evidence and a duty to hear opposing witnesses, can show what ordinary people conclude once they have actually thought the matter through, and its recommendations can then be put to the legislature or to the voters for decision. The lottery's virtue is that it reveals considered public judgement; its limit is that no one has authorised the people it selects to rule.

  1. (A)defend elections against the charge that they systematically keep ordinary citizens such as shift workers and pensioners out of public office.

  3. (C)trace how the ancient Greek preference for filling offices by lot came to be abandoned in favour of the elections that modern states now hold.
  4. (D)show that randomly chosen citizens would legislate more competently than elected politicians and should therefore take their place in most chambers.
Answer and explanation

The purpose is a qualified endorsement of lot as a complement to elections.

Why not (C): The Greek view appears only in paragraph 1 as support for an intuition; the passage gives no history of how lot was abandoned, so this mistakes one detail for the purpose.

Why not (D): Paragraph 3 says only that the competence objection is 'weaker than it sounds', not that lot produces better legislators, and paragraph 4 rejects the idea that lot 'should displace election'.

Common misconception: Taking the strong case for lot in paragraphs 1 and 2 as the whole argument and missing the limit set in paragraphs 3 and 4.

Difficulty: The correct answer depends on holding the endorsement and the concession together; one wrong option captures only the endorsement. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-025-Q4 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following, if true, would most weaken the author's reply to the competence objection in paragraph 3?
  1. (A)Panels chosen by lot, unlike legislators, prove unable to judge which of their expert witnesses are reliable, and tend to adopt the view of whoever spoke last.
  2. (B)Legislators who disappoint their voters are often re-elected anyway, because voters remain loyal to the party under whose name they stand.
  3. (C)Elected legislators who depend heavily on staff and committees are often captured by those advisers, and end up voting as the advisers recommend.

Why not (C): A weakness of relying on advisers would afflict elected and lottery members alike; the reply says only that there is 'no reason a randomly chosen body could not do the same', so a weakness shared by both leaves that parity intact.

Why not (D): The author already allows that legislators are expert in 'a sliver' of what they vote on; expertise in one area is consistent with that and leaves the reliance on advisers untouched.

CAT-VARC-P04-026 · Democracy and participation · passage Easy · 434 words · 4 questions

In a handful of democracies, adults who fail to vote without a good excuse are fined a small sum. To many people elsewhere this looks like a contradiction in terms: surely a free citizen is entitled to stay at home. The objection deserves a hearing, but it is less forceful than it first appears. What such laws typically require is attendance, not a vote for anyone in particular. Ballots remain secret, and a citizen who wishes to register indifference or protest can spoil the ballot paper or leave it blank. The duty imposed is closer to that of serving on a jury or completing a census form than to an order about what to believe. A subtler version of the objection holds that deliberate abstention is itself a political statement, a refusal to lend legitimacy to a contest one regards as empty. Yet a citizen who feels this way loses very little under compulsion, since the blank ballot preserves the statement and arguably sharpens it: a deliberately empty ballot can be counted, whereas a deliberate absence is indistinguishable from the absences of those who merely forgot.

The more interesting question is what compulsion actually changes. Where voting is voluntary, turnout is usually lowest among the young, the poor and the recently arrived, so the electorate that politicians court is older and wealthier than the population as a whole. Campaigns respond rationally to this. A large share of their effort goes into persuading their own likely supporters to turn up, and one of the cheapest ways to do that is to make those supporters angry or afraid about the other side. When almost everyone votes, that strategy loses much of its point. There is little to be gained by stirring up people who will vote anyway, and the votes worth competing for belong to citizens who have not yet made up their minds.

Supporters of compulsion sometimes go further and claim that it produces wiser decisions. Here caution is needed. Being required to attend a polling station does not make anyone better informed, and a citizen who resents the obligation may simply mark the first name on the list. Compulsion enlarges the electorate; it does not educate it. Its real contribution is quieter. It shifts the incentives of those who seek votes, away from rousing the faithful and towards addressing the undecided, and it allows the result to claim to speak for nearly everyone rather than for those who happened to feel strongly enough to come out. That is a modest achievement, but in an age of angry campaigning it is not a small one.

CAT-VARC-P04-027 · Democracy and participation · passage Medium · 446 words · 4 questions

The secret ballot is now so taken for granted that it is easy to forget how recent it is and how fiercely it was once opposed. For much of the nineteenth century, in many countries, voters declared their choices aloud or on papers that others could see, and the reform that replaced this practice was resisted not only by those who profited from intimidation. A respectable strand of liberal opinion held that the vote was a public trust, exercised on behalf of the whole community, and that a trust ought to be discharged in public. A man who knew that his neighbours would see his vote, the argument ran, would be ashamed to cast it for a selfish reason.

The argument has a certain nobility, and it rests on a real insight: people do behave differently when they are observed. Its error lies in assuming that the observer will be the voter's conscience. In practice the people most interested in how a tenant, a labourer or a clerk had voted were the landlord, the employer and the local party agent, and the pressure they applied had little to do with the public good. Publicity did not make votes more responsible; it made them more obedient.

The less obvious virtue of secrecy is that it disarms bribery as well as intimidation. Buying a vote is a kind of contract, and a contract is worthless if one party cannot check whether the other has kept it. Once no one can verify how a ballot was marked, the buyer who pays in advance cannot know whether he has bought anything, and the seller can take the money and vote as he pleases. Secrecy, in other words, works less by appealing to the voter's virtue than by making a certain kind of corruption unenforceable. This also explains why secrecy has to be a duty and not merely a right. If voters could choose to reveal their ballots, those under pressure could be required to prove their compliance, and the protection would collapse for exactly the people who need it most.

That point matters more now than it once did. A ballot marked at home, at a kitchen table, can be watched by a domineering relative; a ballot photographed in a booth with a telephone can be shown to whoever demands proof. Neither practice is sinister in itself, and both make voting easier for many people. But they reopen the gap that secrecy was designed to close, and they suggest that the privacy of the vote is not a natural condition of democratic life but an arrangement that has to be maintained, and occasionally rebuilt, as the means of voting change.

CAT-VARC-P04-027-Q1 · Structure / function · MediumMCQ
The examples of the kitchen table and the telephone in paragraph 4 serve mainly to
  1. (A)suggest that the secret ballot was never as effective in protecting voters as its nineteenth-century supporters had claimed.
  2. (B)support the old liberal view that a vote seen by others is more likely to be cast for the good of the community.
  3. (C)show that newer ways of voting can recreate the exposure to pressure that secrecy was meant to remove.
  4. (D)argue that voting at home and voting with a telephone in the booth should both be prohibited in order to protect the ballot.

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 4 says these practices 'reopen the gap that secrecy was designed to close' and concludes that privacy is 'an arrangement that has to be maintained'. The examples show how changes in the means of voting can expose voters again.

Why not (B): This reverses the point: the observers in paragraph 4 are a 'domineering relative' and 'whoever demands proof', the same kind of pressure paragraph 2 calls obedience, not responsibility.

Why not (D): The author says 'Neither practice is sinister in itself' and that both make voting easier; the conclusion is that secrecy must be maintained, not that these methods must be banned.

Common misconception: Turning the author's call for vigilance into a call for prohibition.

Difficulty: The option about prohibition is tempting because the examples are presented as dangers; the qualification 'Neither practice is sinister in itself' rules it out. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-027-Q4 · Application / analogy · DifficultMCQ
By the reasoning of paragraph 3, which of the following arrangements would best preserve the protection that secrecy gives against vote buying?
  1. (A)Ballots are cast openly, but any voter who reports an attempt to buy a vote is given full legal protection against the person who made it.
  2. (B)Ballots are marked in private, but each voter is handed a numbered slip, and the marked papers are published with their numbers after the count.
  3. (C)Voters may decide whether to keep their ballots secret, and those who choose to disclose their vote receive an official receipt recording the choice.
  4. (D)Voters may photograph their ballots, but any voter may later cast a fresh ballot that silently replaces the earlier one, so no photograph proves the final vote.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

The protection in paragraph 3 rests on one condition: no one 'can verify how a ballot was marked'. If any vote can be silently replaced, a photograph of one ballot proves nothing about the vote that counts, so a buyer still cannot check the bargain. Each other arrangement lets the buyer verify the vote.

Why not (A): An open ballot lets the buyer check the vote, so the bargain is enforceable; legal protection for reporters does not restore the unverifiability that paragraph 3 relies on.

Why not (B): Privacy in the booth is not enough if the vote can be traced afterwards; a published, numbered paper lets a buyer demand the slip and check the vote, which the passage says destroys the protection.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 says that if voters 'could choose to reveal their ballots', those under pressure could be made to prove compliance; an optional receipt does exactly this.

Common misconception: Treating privacy at the moment of marking as the essence of secrecy, rather than the impossibility of verifying the vote afterwards.

Difficulty: The solver must extract the principle of unverifiability and apply it to an unfamiliar arrangement in which a photograph is permitted. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P04-028 · Democracy and participation · passage Difficult · 453 words · 4 questions

Few political terms are used as loosely as populism. It is applied to movements of the left and of the right, to leaders who promise to spend lavishly and to those who promise to cut, to campaigns against banks and to campaigns against immigrants. A word that covers so much risks covering nothing, and some scholars have concluded that it is merely a label for whatever kind of politics the speaker happens to dislike. That conclusion is premature. The variety of populist programmes is real, but it may simply show that populism is not a programme at all.

Consider the most common attempt at definition: populism as hostility to elites. This cannot be sufficient, since criticism of the powerful is the ordinary business of opposition in any democracy, and a definition that made every challenger a populist would be useless. What distinguishes the populist is a further claim, namely that there exists a single, authentic people whose will is already known, and that the movement or leader in question alone gives it voice. Opponents are therefore not merely mistaken; they are illegitimate, and citizens who vote for them are not quite part of the people. On this account the core of populism is not anti-elitism but anti-pluralism, a refusal to accept that the people may be divided in good faith.

The value of this definition is that it explains a pattern which approaches based on policy tend to miss. A movement that claims to embody the whole people has difficulty accepting defeat, since a loss at the polls must then be explained as fraud, manipulation or the work of enemies; and once in office it is tempted to treat independent courts, a critical press or a neutral civil service as obstacles placed between the people and its rightful expression. None of this follows from hostility to elites alone. It follows from the claim to a moral monopoly of representation.

Yet the definition carries a hazard of its own. Precisely because it locates the danger in a style of claim rather than in a set of grievances, it can tempt those who use it to dismiss the grievances along with the style. Many people drawn to populist movements have real complaints: about decisions taken far away from them, about parties that seem interchangeable, about institutions that consult them rarely. To treat such voters as a symptom to be managed, rather than as citizens with reasons, is to deny them the standing that pluralism promises, and to confirm the populist story that an elite has stopped listening. The definition is a tool for recognising a threat to democratic competition; it is not a licence to stop asking why the threat finds an audience.

CAT-VARC-P04-029 · Democracy and participation · passage Medium · 427 words · 4 questions

Political parties have few admirers. It is a commonplace of political commentary that they are distrusted, and reformers of every persuasion have proposed ways of loosening their grip: independent candidates, direct votes on policy, primaries open to any voter who cares to take part. The suspicion is understandable. Parties reward loyalty over judgement, they paper over disagreements in order to win, and their leaders sometimes seem more interested in the survival of the organisation than in the purposes it was founded to serve.

Yet much of what makes democratic government workable is done by parties, and done so routinely that it goes unnoticed. A modern electorate contains millions of people with conflicting interests. Someone has to assemble those interests into a programme coherent enough to be offered, defended and, if elected, attempted; someone has to recruit and test the people who will hold office; and someone has to give voters a simple way of knowing what a candidate broadly stands for without studying the record of each individual. Parties do all three. Most importantly, they make accountability possible. A government formed by a party can be judged as a team at the next election, whereas a legislature of independents, each with a private bargain to defend, offers voters no one in particular to reward or punish.

This is why some reforms intended to democratise parties have had awkward consequences. When candidates are chosen in primaries open to anyone, the party organisation loses much of its ability to screen out those who would damage it, and the candidates who win are often those with the most intense supporters rather than those with the broadest appeal. When membership dwindles, the organisation does not disappear; it becomes a smaller circle of professionals and funders, less rooted in local life and more remote from the voters it claims to represent. Weakening the party, in other words, does not necessarily hand power to citizens. It may hand it instead to whoever is able to organise without one: wealthy individuals, single-issue lobbies, the personal following of a celebrity.

None of this makes parties virtuous. A party that faces no serious competition will serve itself, and the defenders of party government have sometimes mistaken the indispensability of parties for their innocence. The argument here is a narrower one. It is that the familiar complaints about parties are complaints about particular parties behaving badly, and that the cure for bad parties is better ones, more open to their members and more seriously contested, rather than a politics that tries to do without them altogether.

  2. (B)Parties deserve far more admiration than they receive, since the functions they perform show that they are fundamentally virtuous.
  3. (C)Open primaries, though meant to democratise parties, usually produce candidates with intense followings rather than broad appeal.
  4. (D)Parties have become so self-serving that reformers are right to replace them with independent candidates and direct votes on policy.

Paragraph 2 lists what parties do for democratic government, paragraph 3 shows that weakening them shifts power elsewhere, and paragraph 4 concludes that 'the cure for bad parties is better ones'. The correct answer states the claim with its qualification.

Common misconception: Choosing the open-primaries detail, or overstating the defence of parties into praise of their virtue.

CAT-VARC-P04-029-Q3 · Meaning in context · EasyMCQ
When the author says that defenders of party government have 'mistaken the indispensability of parties for their innocence', the point is that these defenders
  1. (A)have confused the innocent intentions of party founders with the conduct of the parties they built.
  2. (B)have wrongly believed that democracy could function perfectly well without any parties at all.
  3. (C)have assumed that because parties are necessary to democracy, they must also be free of blame.
  4. (D)have argued that parties are guilty of misconduct only when they face no serious competition.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

The word 'indispensability' means being necessary; 'innocence' means being free of fault. Paragraph 4 says that the defenders treated the first as if it implied the second, which is why the author adds 'None of this makes parties virtuous'.

Why not (A): The founders' intentions are not discussed; the contrast in the phrase is between being necessary and being blameless.

Why not (B): This reverses the phrase: the defenders overrate parties, believing them necessary and blameless, rather than thinking them dispensable.

Why not (D): The sentence before mentions parties without competition, but the phrase itself describes a confusion between necessity and blamelessness, not a theory of when parties misbehave.

Common misconception: Reading the phrase as being about party founders or as a claim that parties are unnecessary.

Difficulty: The phrase can be decoded directly from its two central words and the sentence that frames it. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-030 · Democracy and participation · passage Easy · 416 words · 4 questions

A common complaint about protest is that it inconveniences people who have done nothing wrong. Commuters are delayed, shops lose trade, ambulances must find another route. The complaint has some force, but it often hides an assumption that deserves to be questioned: that legitimate protest would cause no inconvenience at all. A march that nobody notices, held where nobody has to walk around it, is hardly a protest. Part of the point of gathering in public is to make a claim that others cannot easily ignore.

This is why disruption has always been close to the centre of democratic protest rather than at its edge. Groups without money, office or access to the press have few ways of placing an issue on the public agenda, and a temporary cost imposed on the wider community is one of them. Many causes now regarded as plainly just were first brought to public notice by people who were considered a nuisance, and their critics complained, in much the same terms as are heard today, that they had chosen the wrong methods and the wrong moment.

None of this means that any disruption is acceptable. The useful distinction is between disruption that seeks a hearing and disruption that seeks to impose an outcome. The first imposes a cost in order to make others pay attention; it accepts that, once they have paid attention, they may still disagree. The second tries to make it impossible for others to act on their own decision, for instance by preventing a lawful vote from taking place or by keeping people from a service they are entitled to use. Protest of the first kind is part of the argument on which democracy runs. Protest of the second kind substitutes pressure for persuasion, and a democracy can rightly limit it.

Proportion matters as well. A blockade that delays traffic for an afternoon is different from one that prevents a hospital from receiving supplies, even if both are aimed only at securing attention. Those who protest have a responsibility to choose methods whose costs bear some relation to the importance of what they are saying, and those who govern have a responsibility not to treat every inconvenience as an emergency. Neither responsibility can be reduced to a simple rule, and particular cases will always be argued over; but the alternative to such argument is worse. A society that tolerates no disruption at all has, in effect, decided that only those who already have a hearing deserve one.

According to the passage, disruption is close to the centre of democratic protest because
  1. (A)the public has always come to regard the causes of disruptive protesters as just once their methods are forgotten.
  2. (B)protesters who cause no disruption are usually breaking the law, while those who cause disruption usually are not.
  3. (C)governments in most democracies treat every inconvenience caused by protesters as an emergency requiring a response.
  4. (D)groups lacking money, office or press access have few other ways of bringing an issue to public notice.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 2 explains: 'Groups without money, office or access to the press have few ways of placing an issue on the public agenda, and a temporary cost imposed on the wider community is one of them.'

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 says 'Many causes' now thought just were first raised by nuisances; a claim about every such cause, with a condition about forgotten methods, goes beyond this.

Why not (B): The passage makes no claim about the legality of quiet or disruptive protest; this is outside its scope.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 urges governments not to treat every inconvenience as an emergency; it does not say most governments do, and this is not the reason given in paragraph 2.

Common misconception: Overgeneralising the historical remark about causes later judged just into a reason that is always true.

Difficulty: The reason is stated directly in the second sentence of paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-030-Q4 · Application / analogy · EasyMCQ

CAT-VARC-P04-031 · Democracy and participation · passage Medium · 587 words · 5 questions

When a town loses its newspaper, the loss is usually mourned in the language of community: the obituaries, the school sports results, the notices of births and marriages that no one else will print. These are real losses, but they are not the ones that matter most for self-government. What disappears with the local paper is something duller and more important, namely the habit of attendance. For decades, in many places, a reporter sat through the meetings of the council, the planning committee and the school board, read the budget documents that nobody else read, and noticed when a contract went to the same firm for the third time without a tender.

Most of what such reporters wrote was unremarkable, and much of it was barely read. Its value lay less in any particular story than in the knowledge, shared by every official in the room, that someone was taking notes. Officials who know that a question may be asked in print tomorrow tend to ask it of themselves today. Monitoring of this kind works rather as a speed camera does: its effect is measured not by the fines it issues but by the careless driving it prevents. Remove it, and the immediate result is not a wave of scandal, since there is no longer anyone to report one, but a gradual loosening of the restraint that observation imposed. The absence of bad news, in a town without a reporter, is not evidence of good government.

It is often said that the internet has more than made up the difference, since anyone can now publish and residents can share information instantly. This confuses the supply of commentary with the supply of attention. A neighbourhood forum can spread a rumour about the council in minutes, but it seldom has anyone willing to spend three evenings a month in council meetings, or the training to read a set of accounts. National outlets, for their part, cover local government only when it produces a story large enough to interest a national audience, which is usually to say only after the damage is done. What the internet has multiplied is reaction; what has thinned out is the patient, unglamorous observation on which informed reaction depends.

None of this should be allowed to turn into nostalgia. Many local papers were timid with powerful advertisers, cosy with the town's leading figures and given to boosterism, celebrating every new development while asking few questions about who would profit from it. A press that depends on the goodwill of the people it covers is a weak watchdog, and some of the papers now lamented were weak in exactly this way, flattering the very people they ought to have been questioning. The case for local journalism does not rest on the virtue of the institutions that happened to provide it.

It rests, rather, on the function those institutions performed, however imperfectly. If that function is to survive the collapse of the business model that once paid for it, it will have to be supported deliberately: by newsrooms funded as public goods, by universities that send students to cover meetings, perhaps by public records published in forms that a busy citizen can actually read. Each of these options carries hazards, and money from the very governments that journalists are meant to watch carries the most obvious one. But a democracy that leaves the observation of its local governments to chance should not be surprised when those governments begin to behave as if no one is looking.

CAT-VARC-P04-032 · Democracy and participation · passage Difficult · 585 words · 5 questions

That most citizens know little about the policies they vote on is one of the most frequently confirmed observations about modern democracies, and one of the least surprising. A single vote almost never decides an election, and the hours needed to master the details of tax law or trade policy are hours taken from work, family and sleep. Ignorance, in these circumstances, is not a failure of character. It is what a sensible person would choose if he or she treated the vote as a decision with private costs and a vanishingly small chance of making a difference. Economists call this rational ignorance, and the label is apt: the problem is not that voters reason badly but that they have little reason to acquire what good reasoning would need.

Two very different conclusions are drawn from this. The first, which has gained a certain academic following, is that the franchise should be weighted towards those who know more, since a decision made by the informed is likely to be better than one made by everyone. The second, more common among defenders of democracy, is that ignorance matters less than it seems, because voters rely on shortcuts. They need not study a programme if they know which party proposed it; they need not understand monetary policy to notice whether prices are rising; they can follow the advice of an organisation they trust. A voter equipped with such cues, the argument goes, can vote much as she would if she were fully informed.

The second conclusion is more attractive than the first, but it claims too much. A shortcut is only as good as the connection between the cue and the thing the voter actually cares about, and that connection is not guaranteed. Party labels are informative when parties are stable and their programmes distinct; when parties fragment or converge, the label says little. Judging a government by how things are going is reasonable if the government controls how things are going, but voters who punish an incumbent for a drought, or for a downturn that began abroad, are using a cue that measures luck as much as competence. And an endorsement is valuable only if the endorser shares the voter's interests, which is precisely what a well-funded campaign may try to disguise. Shortcuts, in short, do not make ignorance harmless; they make its effects depend on the environment in which voters find themselves.

That dependence, however, is also the strongest reply to those who would ration the vote. If the quality of popular judgement varies with the reliability of the cues available, then the natural remedy is to improve the cues rather than to disqualify the people who use them. Parties with clear and lasting identities, intermediaries whose interests are visible, a press that reports what governments actually control: each of these makes ordinary shortcuts more trustworthy. Weighting votes by knowledge, by contrast, would leave the environment of cues untouched while inviting a quarrel, unlikely ever to be settled impartially, about who should set the test and what it should contain.

Neither side, then, has quite grasped the problem. The critics are right that an uninformed electorate can be misled, and the defenders are right that citizens need not be experts to judge well. What both overlook is that the competence of voters is not a fixed property of individuals but partly a product of institutions, and that a democracy can therefore make its citizens wiser as voters without making any of them more learned.

CAT-VARC-P04-032-Q1 · Meaning in context · MediumMCQ
As used in paragraph 1, 'rational ignorance' refers to

  2. (B)ignorance that voters conceal by offering reasonable-sounding justifications for choices they made without any real thought.
  3. (C)a lack of knowledge that comes from sensibly weighing the cost of learning against a vote's tiny chance of mattering.
  4. (D)knowledge that governments deliberately withhold from voters in order to avoid the scrutiny that an informed public would bring.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 1 explains that ignorance 'is what a sensible person would choose if he or she treated the vote as a decision with private costs and a vanishingly small chance of making a difference', and that 'Economists call this rational ignorance'.

Why not (B): Nothing in paragraph 1 concerns concealment or justification; the term describes why voters choose not to acquire knowledge.

Why not (D): The ignorance described is chosen by voters themselves in view of costs; government secrecy is not mentioned anywhere in the passage.

Common misconception: Reading 'rational' as describing how voters reason about what they know, rather than the decision not to learn.

Difficulty: The term is defined by the surrounding sentences, but one wrong option exploits the ordinary sense of 'rational'. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-032-Q5 · Main idea / primary purpose · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following best states the author's overall position?
  1. (A)Since shortcuts are too unreliable to depend on, democracies should do much more to make every citizen properly informed about policy.
  2. (B)Since voters' ignorance is rational, it poses no real threat to the quality of democratic decisions and requires no remedy.
  3. (C)Since uninformed voters can easily be misled, the critics of democracy are right to give greater weight to the votes of informed citizens.
  4. (D)Since voters' competence depends partly on institutions, democracies should make the cues voters rely on more reliable instead of restricting who may vote.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 4 says 'the natural remedy is to improve the cues rather than to disqualify the people who use them', and paragraph 5 adds that competence is 'partly a product of institutions'. The correct answer joins the two.

Why not (A): Paragraph 5 says a democracy can make citizens 'wiser as voters without making any of them more learned', so the remedy is better cues, not more knowledge.

Why not (B): Paragraph 3 says shortcuts 'do not make ignorance harmless', and paragraph 5 grants that 'an uninformed electorate can be misled'.

Why not (C): The author accepts the first clause in paragraph 5 but rejects the conclusion in paragraph 4, so this combines a concession with a view the author opposes.

Common misconception: Choosing the education option, which shares the author's dissatisfaction with shortcuts but proposes the remedy the final sentence rejects.

Difficulty: Every wrong option uses a premise the author accepts; the solver must match the remedy as well as the diagnosis. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P04-033 · War, peace and world order · passage Easy · 448 words · 4 questions

Neutrality has a poor reputation among people who think seriously about war. To its critics it looks like a refusal to choose, a way for a small state to sit out the great moral contests of its time while others pay the price of deciding them. On this view the neutral is a free rider at best and a quiet collaborator at worst, trading with whichever side is winning and calling its caution a principle. The charge is not baseless, but it rests on a misunderstanding of what neutrality has usually been for the states that practised it with any success.

For a small country surrounded by larger ones, neutrality is less a moral posture than a strategy, and a demanding one. Its purpose is to remove the country from the calculations of its neighbours, to persuade each of them that the territory will never be used as a road or a base by the others. That persuasion does not come from declarations. It has to be earned through a long record of evenhanded conduct, through refusing favours that would look like alignment, and, above all, through an army strong enough to make occupation expensive. The neutral that cannot defend its borders is not neutral in any meaningful sense; it is simply unclaimed, and unclaimed ground in wartime is soon claimed.

Seen this way, neutrality is not passivity but a continuous effort of a particular kind. The neutral state must be ready to resist whichever side violates its frontier first, which means it must arm against all of its neighbours at once rather than against one. It must also be disciplined in peacetime, since a single treaty of friendship, a preferential trade deal or a careless remark by a minister can be read abroad as a signal of where it would stand in a crisis. Few alliances ask as much of their members as this solitary commitment asks of a country that has chosen it.

None of this guarantees success. Neutrality works only so long as the great powers judge that respecting it costs them less than violating it, and when a neutral's territory becomes essential to someone's war plans, even a well-armed and scrupulous record may count for little. Nor does the strategic defence settle every moral question; a neutral that profits handsomely from a war it claims to stand apart from invites the suspicion its critics voice. But the critics usually ask the wrong question. They ask whether neutrality is admirable, when the more useful question is whether, for a particular state in a particular place, it is the course most likely to keep that state's people out of a war they could not win.
