  1. (A)explain why small states usually prefer forming alliances with one another to relying on the protection of a great power.
  2. (B)show that neutral states have nearly always stayed out of wars, provided that they kept armies strong enough to deter invasion.
  3. (C)recast neutrality, which critics treat as moral evasion, as a costly strategy by which small states try to keep out of wars.
  4. (D)defend neutral states against the charge of profiteering by showing that very few of them have gained from wars nearby.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 1 states the critics' charge and says it 'rests on a misunderstanding'. Paragraphs 2 and 3 present neutrality as 'a strategy, and a demanding one' requiring armed strength and peacetime discipline, and paragraph 4 qualifies the claim while redirecting the question to whether neutrality keeps a state out of war. The purpose is to recast neutrality as a costly strategy.

Why not (A): Alliances appear only in a comparison in paragraph 3 ('Few alliances ask as much'); the passage never discusses small states allying with one another.

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 opens 'None of this guarantees success' and says even a well-armed record 'may count for little', so the claim of near-constant success contradicts the author.

Why not (D): The author concedes in paragraph 4 that a neutral that profits 'invites the suspicion its critics voice'; nowhere does the passage claim such profit is rare.

Common misconception: Turning the author's defence into a claim of guaranteed success, ignoring the concession in paragraph 4.

CAT-VARC-P04-034 · War, peace and world order · passage Easy · 427 words · 4 questions

Few historical lessons are repeated as confidently as the one drawn from the settlement that followed the First World War. A harsh peace, the lesson runs, breeds resentment, and resentment breeds the next war; a generous peace, by contrast, turns yesterday's enemy into tomorrow's partner. The lesson is attractive because it joins morality to prudence. It tells victors that magnanimity is not only noble but wise, and that they can secure the future simply by being kind. It also offers a single, memorable explanation for one of the great catastrophes of the last century, and explanations of that kind are hard to dislodge once they have settled into textbooks and speeches.

The trouble is that the record does not sort so neatly. Some severe settlements have held for generations, because the defeated party was too weak to overturn them and the victors remained willing to enforce their terms. Some lenient ones have failed, because the loser read leniency as a sign that it had not really been beaten and that a second attempt might succeed. What such cases suggest is that severity and generosity are the wrong variables to watch. A settlement lasts when its terms match the actual balance of strength, and when the powers that wrote it remain prepared to uphold it.

By this measure, the worst settlement is not the harshest but the one that combines harsh terms with a weak will to enforce them. Such a peace gives the defeated every reason to resent the order and no reason to fear challenging it. Its demands are heavy enough to humiliate, yet the victors, tired of war and divided among themselves, let them be evaded piece by piece, until the settlement has lost both its authority and its power to deter. Much of the interwar story can be told in this way, as a failure less of severity than of follow-through.

This is not an argument for cruelty dressed up as realism. Generosity has a real role, but it works best after the defeat has been made unmistakable, when it can be read as the confidence of a winner rather than the hesitation of a power unsure of its victory. Offering a defeated state a path back into the community of nations, through trade, membership of institutions and the eventual lifting of restrictions, gives it a stake in the order that no amount of punishment can create. The point is only that kindness must rest on a settlement that is clear about who won and is backed by someone willing to say so.

CAT-VARC-P04-034-Q1 · Structure / function · EasyMCQ
Paragraph 2 functions in the passage chiefly to
  1. (A)offer cases that the popular lesson cannot explain and to propose different factors that decide whether a settlement lasts.
  2. (B)prove that severe settlements are, as a rule, more durable than lenient ones and should therefore be preferred by victors.
  3. (C)describe in detail how the terms of the settlement after the First World War came to be evaded one by one.
  4. (D)explain why victorious powers find it comforting to believe that a generous peace is both noble and prudent.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 2 opens 'the record does not sort so neatly', gives severe settlements that held and lenient ones that failed, and concludes that a settlement lasts when its terms 'match the actual balance of strength' and are upheld. It supplies counter-cases and an alternative explanation.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 says severity and generosity 'are the wrong variables to watch', so it does not rank harsh settlements above lenient ones.

Why not (C): The evasion of harsh terms 'piece by piece' is described in paragraph 3, and paragraph 2 gives no detail about that settlement.

Why not (D): The appeal of the lesson, that it 'joins morality to prudence', is explained in paragraph 1, not paragraph 2.

Common misconception: Reading the mention of durable severe settlements as an argument for severity.

Difficulty: The paragraph's role is announced by its first sentence and summed up by its last. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-035 · War, peace and world order · passage Medium · 436 words · 4 questions

Economic sanctions are usually defended with a simple theory of pressure: cut a country off from markets, credit and imported goods, and its people will suffer; suffering people will blame their rulers; and rulers who fear their own people will eventually change course. Each link in this chain sounds plausible, and the whole has the further appeal of offering governments something to do that is stronger than protest and safer than war, a way of being seen to act without asking anyone to fight. Yet the chain breaks more often than its defenders admit, and it tends to break in the same place.

The weak link is the assumption that hardship flows upward as political pressure. In a country whose government controls the press and the police, scarcity is easier to explain than to resist. The regime can present the shortages as the work of hostile foreigners, and the explanation is the more convincing for being partly true. Scarcity also hands the state new powers, which it is seldom slow to use. When imported medicine, fuel or spare parts become rare, whoever controls their distribution acquires a means of rewarding loyalty and punishing dissent. Meanwhile the groups best placed to demand change, the educated and the commercially minded, are often the first to emigrate or to be impoverished into silence. Far from loosening the regime's grip, sanctions can tighten it.

The usual response is to make sanctions smarter: to target the assets and travel of named officials rather than the economy as a whole. This is a genuine improvement, since it spares ordinary people some of the cost and aims the pain at those who actually decide. But targeted measures have limits of their own, and those limits are not trivial. Powerful individuals rarely hold their wealth in their own names, and a regime under pressure learns quickly to route its money through intermediaries. Targeting reduces the moral cost of sanctions more reliably than it increases their effect.

What the record suggests is that sanctions are often judged by the wrong measure. Their defenders point to the damage done, the fall in output and the collapse of the currency, as if these were evidence of pressure. They are evidence only of pain, and pain is not the same as leverage. Sanctions change policy when they alter the calculations of the people who make it, which usually requires that they be paired with a clear and credible offer of what will be lifted in return for what. A measure that hurts but promises nothing gives a government little reason to move, and some reason to dig in.

CAT-VARC-P04-036 · War, peace and world order · passage Medium · 434 words · 4 questions

Among the arguments made for nuclear deterrence, the strongest is also the simplest: when two states can each destroy the other, neither can hope to gain from a general war, and so neither starts one. Whatever its moral costs, a balance of terror of this kind has a claim to have kept the largest powers from fighting each other directly for a long time. But the logic that makes the largest war unthinkable may make smaller ones more thinkable, and this possibility, sometimes called the stability-instability paradox, deserves more attention than the reassuring half of the argument usually receives.

The reasoning runs as follows. If both sides are confident that neither will ever risk a nuclear exchange, then each can assume that a limited clash, whether a border skirmish, a proxy war or a raid by irregular forces, will stay limited. The fear that once restrained such ventures, the fear that they might spiral into catastrophe, is weakened precisely by the confidence that catastrophe will be avoided. Stability at the top of the ladder of escalation thus loosens the rungs near the bottom. A state that would never dare a full invasion may feel free to probe, provoke and nibble, reasoning that its opponent will not answer with anything that could lead to the unthinkable.

It is important to be precise about what this claims. It does not say that nuclear weapons cause small wars, which have many causes and were common long before such weapons existed. It says only that mutual deterrence can lower the perceived risk of low-level aggression and so remove one brake on it. Nor does the paradox operate automatically. Its force depends on how confident each side is that escalation can be controlled. Where leaders doubt that a skirmish can be kept small, the old fear returns, and with it some of the old caution.

The implication is uncomfortable for those who credit deterrence with keeping the peace. What deterrence may secure is not peace in general but the absence of one particular kind of war, while the violence that remains is pushed downward and outward, into contested borderlands and into the hands of proxies. That is no small achievement, given what the alternative might be, and nothing in the paradox implies that the world would be safer if the largest powers could once again contemplate fighting each other directly. But the achievement should be described accurately. A system that prevents catastrophe partly by making lesser conflicts safer to start is not a system of peace; it is a way of choosing which wars will be fought.

  4. (D)how many nuclear weapons each side possesses in comparison with the arsenal of its rival.

Why not (D): The size of arsenals is never discussed; the passage assumes only that the two states 'can each destroy the other'.

Common misconception: Picking a detail mentioned in paragraph 3 for another purpose.

CAT-VARC-P04-036-Q3 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · MediumMCQ
Which of the following, if true, would most strengthen the reasoning set out in paragraph 2?
  1. (A)Two rival states fought several border wars against each other in the decades before either of them acquired nuclear weapons.
  2. (B)Several non-nuclear states bordering a nuclear power reduced the size of their armies in the years after it acquired its arsenal.
  3. (C)After two rivals acquired secure nuclear arsenals, raids across their border grew more frequent, with officials on both sides expecting the raids to stay contained.
  4. (D)Soon after two rival states acquired nuclear weapons, a minor border incident between them led within hours to a nuclear alert on both sides.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 2 argues that confidence that neither side will risk a nuclear exchange leads each to assume limited clashes 'will stay limited', so it feels freer to provoke. More frequent raids after mutual deterrence, accompanied by stated confidence that they would stay contained, fits both the behaviour and the reasoning behind it.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 grants that small wars 'were common long before such weapons existed'; earlier wars say nothing about whether deterrence weakens restraint.

Why not (B): The paragraph concerns two states that can each destroy the other; troop cuts by non-nuclear neighbours do not bear on that mutual reasoning.

Why not (D): Rapid escalation would undermine the confidence the paradox needs, and so would tend to restore caution rather than support the reasoning.

Common misconception: Choosing an option about border wars in general without checking whether it involves the confidence that drives the paradox.

Difficulty: Requires matching both the change in behaviour and the belief behind it. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-036-Q4 · Inference · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following can most reasonably be inferred from the passage?
  1. (A)Small wars in the nuclear age are caused mainly by the confidence that mutual deterrence gives to the leaders of rival nuclear-armed states.
  2. (B)The author regards nuclear deterrence as a failure, since it has not succeeded in removing organised violence between rival states.
  3. (C)The paradox shows that the largest powers have in fact become more willing to fight each other directly once both are armed.
  4. (D)Where leaders doubt that clashes can be contained, deterrence is likely to weaken restraint on low-level aggression less than where they are confident of control.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 3 says the paradox does not 'operate automatically' and that where leaders doubt a skirmish can be kept small, 'the old fear returns, and with it some of the old caution'. So under such doubt the loosening of restraint will be smaller than where leaders are confident that escalation can be controlled.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 says explicitly that the paradox 'does not say that nuclear weapons cause small wars', only that deterrence can remove 'one brake' on them.

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 calls the prevention of catastrophe 'no small achievement'; the author asks only that it be described accurately, not that it be judged a failure.

Why not (C): Paragraph 1 says deterrence has a claim to have kept the largest powers from fighting 'each other directly'; the paradox concerns lesser conflicts.

Common misconception: Choosing the causal option, which overstates the paradox in exactly the way paragraph 3 warns against.

CAT-VARC-P04-037 · War, peace and world order · passage Difficult · 495 words · 4 questions

The laws of war rest on a premise that, stated baldly, can seem scandalous. A soldier who fights for an aggressor and a soldier who fights to repel him are held to have exactly the same rights on the battlefield: each may lawfully kill enemy combatants, each must spare civilians and prisoners, and neither commits a crime merely by taking part. Whether the war itself was just, a question settled, if at all, by the cause for which it was begun, is treated as irrelevant to how it may be fought. The justice of going to war and justice in the conduct of war are kept in separate compartments, and the ordinary soldier is answerable only for the second.

Philosophical critics have pressed hard on this separation, and their central argument is difficult to resist. Outside war, we do not think that someone who attacks without justification acquires a right to kill those who resist him; a robber who is fired on by the guard he is trying to overpower may not shoot back and call it self-defence. Why should the matter change when the attacker wears a uniform and acts on orders? If the right to kill in war derives, as it must, from the right to defend against an unjust threat, then those who pose the unjust threat seem to have no such right at all. On this view, the soldier of an aggressor state who kills a defending soldier does wrong, however scrupulously he observes the rules of conduct.

I think the critics are right about the underlying morality and wrong about what follows for the laws of war. Rules for the conduct of hostilities must be followed by people who are frightened, poorly informed and dependent on their own government's account of why they are fighting. Nearly every belligerent believes, or is told, that its cause is just. A rule that granted the right to kill only to soldiers on the just side would therefore be claimed by both sides, and would offer no restraint precisely where restraint is most needed; worse, it would invite each side to treat captured enemies as criminals rather than as prisoners. The symmetry of the laws of war is not a judgement that both sides are equally innocent. It is a convention adopted because the alternative would make war more savage without making it more just.

Conceding this does not license the conclusion that soldiers bear no responsibility for the wars they fight. A convention that withholds legal blame is not a certificate of moral innocence, and there will be wars whose injustice is so plain that an ordinary soldier could see it and ought to refuse. The doctrine of equality is best understood not as a truth about the morality of killing but as a practical arrangement that we maintain, knowingly, because it does more to limit the horror of war than any rule that tracked the moral truth more closely could do.

CAT-VARC-P04-037-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · MediumMCQ
Which of the following best expresses the central claim of the passage?
  1. (A)The laws of war should be revised so that soldiers who fight for an aggressor can be prosecuted for the killing of those who defend against them.
  2. (B)The critics of the laws of war are mistaken, since a soldier acting on lawful orders cannot fairly be compared with a robber acting on his own.
  3. (C)Because nearly every belligerent believes its cause just, the morality of a war cannot be judged, and its soldiers bear no moral responsibility for it.
  4. (D)Although soldiers fighting for an aggressor may lack a real moral right to kill, the laws of war are wise to treat combatants on both sides alike.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 3 states the thesis: the critics 'are right about the underlying morality and wrong about what follows for the laws of war'. The symmetry is 'a convention' justified because the alternative would make war more savage, and paragraph 4 adds that it is not a judgement of innocence.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 argues that such a rule would 'make war more savage without making it more just'; the author defends the existing symmetry.

Why not (B): The author says 'the critics are right about the underlying morality'; the disagreement concerns only what follows for the law, not the robber comparison.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 says the convention 'is not a certificate of moral innocence' and that soldiers sometimes 'ought to refuse', so the author does not relieve them of responsibility.

Common misconception: Choosing the option that rejects the critics outright, missing that the author accepts their moral argument.

Difficulty: The thesis concedes one point and denies another; the wrong options each keep only one half. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

The author would most likely regard a soldier who knowingly fights in a plainly aggressive war, while observing every rule of conduct, as
  1. (A)free of legal blame under the convention, but not for that reason morally innocent of the killing that he does.
  2. (B)morally blameless for as long as he obeyed his orders, since orders transfer responsibility to the government that issued them.
  3. (C)guilty of a crime under the laws of war, since those laws ought in principle to follow the justice of the cause.

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 4 adds that the convention 'is not a certificate of moral innocence' and that where injustice is 'so plain that an ordinary soldier could see it' he 'ought to refuse'. Legal blamelessness and moral fault are kept apart.

Why not (B): The passage never says orders transfer responsibility; paragraph 4 says that in a plainly unjust war an ordinary soldier 'ought to refuse'.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 defends laws that do not follow the justice of the cause, so the author would not call this soldier a criminal.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 says a convention that 'withholds legal blame is not a certificate of moral innocence', so the author keeps moral blame in view.

Common misconception: Collapsing the author's distinction between legal blame and moral innocence in one direction or the other.

CAT-VARC-P04-037-Q4 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · DifficultMCQ
The author's argument in paragraph 3 for treating combatants alike depends most on which of the following assumptions?
  1. (A)Most wars are fought between sides whose causes are, on a fair reckoning, roughly equal in their justice.
  2. (B)No authority exists whose ruling on which side has the just cause would be accepted by the belligerents while the fighting continues.

CAT-VARC-P04-038 · War, peace and world order · passage Medium · 446 words · 4 questions

Collective security is one of those ideas that seem obviously right until one asks what they require. The principle is that an attack on any member of an international system is to be treated as an attack on all, so that every aggressor faces the combined strength of everyone else. Against this, the older practice of the balance of power looks cynical: states align against whichever power threatens to dominate, and they change sides as readily as the threat shifts, guided by interest rather than by any judgement about who is in the right.

The appeal of collective security lies in its impartiality, and so does its weakness. For the scheme to work, states must be willing to fight an aggressor regardless of who it is, which means fighting, if necessary, a friend, a major trading partner or a power whose goodwill they need elsewhere. They must also be willing to fight over places where they have no interest at all, because the principle admits no distinction between an attack that matters to them and one that does not. This is a great deal to ask of governments that answer to their own citizens, and the interwar experience suggests that, when the moment came, many would not pay the price. An organisation founded on the principle found its members ready to condemn aggression and reluctant to resist it.

The balance of power asks less and so delivers more reliably, at least within its narrower aims. It does not require states to care about aggression as such, only about the rise of a power that could threaten them, and so it draws on motives that governments actually have. But its reliability comes at a price. Because it attends only to the distribution of strength, it is indifferent to the fate of small states whose conquest does not upset the balance; it can tolerate, and has often arranged, the partition of a weak country as a way of keeping the strong ones satisfied. Stability is its aim, and justice enters only by accident.

The lesson is not that one principle should replace the other, but that each supplies what the other lacks. A norm against aggression, however imperfectly enforced, gives resistance to an expanding power a public justification and makes it easier to assemble allies who would hesitate to act on calculation alone. Balancing, in turn, supplies the motive that the norm cannot generate by itself. Orders that have lasted have usually combined the two, borrowing their legitimacy from the first and their enforcement from the second, and the mistake of the reformers who championed collective security was to imagine that legitimacy could do the work of both.

  2. (B)the other state is a close trading partner whose goodwill it needs in some other region.
  3. (C)the other state's growing strength threatens to let it dominate the states around it.
  4. (D)the other state has attacked a small neighbour without any justification that others accept.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 1 says states under a balance of power 'align against whichever power threatens to dominate', and paragraph 3 says the practice cares 'only about the rise of a power that could threaten them'.

Why not (A): Formal condemnation belongs to the collective-security organisation of paragraph 2, whose members condemned aggression but did not resist it.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 lists such partners as states collective security might require one to fight; under balancing this would be a reason to hold back, not to join.

Why not (D): Paragraph 3 says the balance of power is 'indifferent to the fate of small states whose conquest does not upset the balance', so aggression alone does not trigger it.

Common misconception: Confusing the trigger of collective security (any aggression) with the trigger of balancing (a threat of dominance).

Difficulty: The answer restates paragraph 1, and the wrong options are clearly tied to the other principle. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-038-Q4 · Application / analogy · DifficultMCQ

  1. (A)States already uneasy at a neighbour's military growth unite against it once it seizes a border province, citing a shared rule against conquest to win wider support.
  2. (B)Two great powers agree to divide a weak buffer state between them so that neither gains an advantage, and the arrangement keeps the peace between them for a generation.

CAT-VARC-P04-039 · War, peace and world order · passage Difficult · 577 words · 5 questions

For much of the modern period the doctrine of sovereignty was understood as a wall. Within its borders a state was answerable to no outside authority, and what it did to its own population, however terrible, was a matter of domestic jurisdiction. The rule had a defensible rationale. A world in which any power could enter another's territory whenever it disapproved of how that territory was governed would be a world of permanent intervention, in which the strong would always find reasons to correct the weak. Non-interference was less a tribute to the virtue of governments than a device for keeping their quarrels within bounds.

The atrocities of the late twentieth century made the wall look like a shield for murderers, and a new formula emerged to reconcile the old principle with the demand to act. Sovereignty, it was argued, is not a privilege but a responsibility: a state holds it on condition that it protects its people, and a state that massacres them, or cannot stop others from doing so, forfeits its claim to be left alone. The formula is elegant, because it seems to dissolve the conflict rather than adjudicate it. Intervention to stop mass killing no longer violates sovereignty, since the sovereignty in question has already been forfeited.

The elegance is partly an illusion. The formula does not remove the hard question but relocates it. Someone must still decide when a government has failed badly enough to forfeit its rights, and in practice that someone is usually the state or coalition proposing to intervene, which is to say a party with interests of its own in the outcome. The old doctrine at least gave a clear answer to the question of who may enter: nobody. The new one replaces that answer with a judgement, and judgements made by the powerful about the weak have a history that should make anyone cautious. It is not a cynical point that intervention has tended to occur where the interveners had strategic reasons to be and not elsewhere; it is an observation about how a discretionary rule will be used.

None of this is an argument for returning to the wall. A principle that obliged the world to watch a massacre it could stop would purchase order at a price few would accept once they had seen what it meant. The question is not whether sovereignty may ever yield, but how the decision that it should yield is to be made credible. Here the procedure matters as much as the facts. An intervention authorised by a broad body of states, through rules that apply to friends as well as to enemies, carries a legitimacy that one undertaken by a single power on its own assessment cannot, even when the atrocities alleged are equally real.

The difficulty is that procedure can fail in the other direction. The bodies most able to confer legitimacy are also those in which a single interested member can block action, and the insistence on proper authorisation can become a reason for doing nothing while the killing continues. There is no formula that escapes this. A world that refuses all unauthorised intervention will sometimes stand by when it should act, and a world that permits it will sometimes see intervention used as a pretext. The honest position is to admit that we are choosing which of these failures to risk, and to stop pretending that a change of vocabulary has spared us the choice.

  3. (C)proposing that no use of force across a border be permitted until a broad body of states has authorised it under common rules.
  4. (D)showing that interventions to stop mass killing have generally been pretexts used by powerful states to pursue their strategic interests.
Answer and explanation

Paragraph 3 states that the formula 'does not remove the hard question but relocates it' to who decides, and paragraphs 4 and 5 explore how procedure might answer that and how it too can fail, ending with the admission that 'we are choosing which of these failures to risk'. The concern throughout is that the new formula leaves the hard choice in place.

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 begins 'None of this is an argument for returning to the wall', so the author does not defend the old doctrine.

Why not (C): Paragraph 5 warns that insisting on authorisation 'can become a reason for doing nothing', and the author makes no such proposal.

Why not (D): Paragraph 3 notes a pattern in where interventions occur, but the author accepts that atrocities can be 'equally real' and that standing by can be wrong; calling them generally pretexts overstates this.

Difficulty: Each wrong option matches one paragraph; the correct answer requires the arc of the whole passage. · Exams: CAT, XAT

Common misconception: Linking the phrase to the nearest discussion, of authorisation, instead of to the formula the whole passage examines.

CAT-VARC-P04-040 · War, peace and world order · passage Medium · 596 words · 5 questions

Every alliance contains a quiet bargain between two fears. A state that joins an alliance worries, on the one hand, that its partner will fail it when it is attacked: this is the fear of abandonment. It worries, on the other, that its partner will drag it into a war it has no wish to fight, over interests that are not its own: this is the fear of entrapment. The two fears pull in opposite directions, and much of what passes for the routine management of alliances, the ministerial visits, the joint statements, the careful wording of guarantees, is an effort to keep them in tolerable balance.

The difficulty is that almost anything done to ease one fear aggravates the other. A state that tightens its commitment, by stationing troops on its partner's soil or promising automatic assistance, reassures the partner that it will not be abandoned. But the same assurance may embolden the partner to take risks it would otherwise avoid, confident that its ally will be obliged to follow. Loosening the commitment has the opposite effect: it restrains the partner, but it also invites the partner to doubt the alliance and perhaps to look for other friends, or to arm in ways that alarm its neighbours. There is no setting of the dial at which both fears disappear, and governments that search for one tend to lurch between them, reassuring an anxious ally one year and reining in an emboldened one the next.

For this reason, the ambiguity of many alliance commitments, often criticised as a failure of nerve, is better seen as a deliberate instrument. A guarantee that is firm enough to deter an adversary but vague about the circumstances in which it will be honoured leaves the protecting power room to judge whether its partner has provoked the crisis. The partner, uncertain whether reckless conduct will be supported, has reason to be careful; the adversary, uncertain whether aggression will be resisted, has reason to be careful too. Consider a hypothetical great power that promises to defend an island partner but declines to say whether the promise covers a dispute the partner itself might start. The silence is not an oversight. It is the policy.

The balance between the two fears is rarely the same for both members. A small state facing a powerful neighbour usually fears abandonment far more than entrapment, since it has few other places to turn, while its larger protector, with interests elsewhere and wars it would prefer not to fight, tends to fear entrapment more. Much of the friction within alliances that look harmonious from outside comes from this asymmetry: the junior partner asks for firmer pledges, the senior partner resists giving them, and each suspects the other of bad faith when each is in fact responding sensibly to its own position.

Ambiguity is not free, however. Its value depends on each party reading the uncertainty in the way the protecting power intends, and there is no guarantee that they will. An adversary may conclude that a vague commitment is a hollow one and test it, discovering too late that it was real. A partner may conclude that it will be supported whatever it does, because it cannot imagine its patron allowing it to fall. Deliberate vagueness thus trades the visible risks of a firm commitment for the hidden risk of miscalculation, which is the more dangerous for being invisible until a crisis reveals it. The strategy rewards a protecting power that understands both its partner and its adversary well, and punishes one that merely hopes it does.

CAT-VARC-P04-040-Q1 · Inference · EasyMCQ
The passage suggests that a state fearing entrapment would be most worried that
  1. (A)its adversary might decide that the alliance's commitment is hollow and choose to test it.
  2. (B)its partner might fail to come to its aid if the state itself were attacked by a third power.
  3. (C)its partner, sure of being supported, might start a dispute that the state would then be obliged to join.
  4. (D)its partner might begin looking for other friends and arming itself in ways that alarm its neighbours.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 1 defines entrapment as the fear that the partner 'will drag it into a war it has no wish to fight', and paragraph 2 explains that assurance 'may embolden the partner to take risks'.

Why not (A): Paragraph 5 raises this as a risk of ambiguity concerning the adversary; entrapment is a fear about one's own partner.

Why not (B): This is the fear of abandonment, defined in paragraph 1 as the worry that the partner 'will fail it when it is attacked'.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 gives these as consequences of loosening a commitment, which is what a state does to reduce entrapment, not the entrapment itself.

Common misconception: Swapping the definitions of the two fears.

Difficulty: Definitions are given in one paragraph; only the matching is tested. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-041 · Everyday life in history · passage Easy · 465 words · 4 questions

It is tempting to picture the great periodic fair of the pre-modern world as a market that happened to be very large. Merchants arrived with bales of cloth, casks and livestock; buyers came from the surrounding districts; for a few weeks a meadow outside a town became a crowded street of stalls, and then it emptied again until the following year. On this view the fair existed because trade was too thin to support permanent shops, and it faded once towns grew large enough to sustain them. The account is not wrong, but it describes the visible part of the fair and mistakes it for the whole.

Much of what happened at a fair left no bales behind. Merchants who met there once or twice a year used the occasion to settle accounts that had been running since the previous meeting, cancelling what one owed another against what a third owed the first, so that relatively little coin had to change hands. Promises to pay at the next fair circulated as a kind of credit, and their value rested on the expectation that everyone would return. A fair, in this sense, was a calendar as much as a place: it fixed the dates on which obligations fell due and gave distant traders a reason to keep faith with one another. A merchant who failed to appear risked more than a season's sales; he risked his standing with every partner who had expected to meet him.

It was also a clearing house for news. Prices in distant regions, the solvency of a trading house, a harvest failure, rumours of war: all of this travelled with the people who came, and a merchant who stayed at home was not only missing customers but trading blind. The gossip of the fair was not idle. It was the means by which a scattered commercial world kept a rough common picture of itself. A thinly attended fair was therefore a loss twice over, since the absence of buyers was also an absence of reports, and those who controlled the best information often profited more than those who carried the most goods.

Seen this way, the decline of the great fairs looks different. Permanent shops certainly took over much of the retail business, but the deeper loss was of the fair's other functions, which passed to institutions that did not depend on bodily presence: banks that kept accounts all year round, regular postal services and, later, printed lists of prices. None of this means that fairs ceased to matter as markets; many survived for centuries as places to buy animals or household goods. What they lost was their role as the hinge on which a whole year of commerce turned, and that role had never been visible in the stalls.

CAT-VARC-P04-041-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · EasyMCQ

  2. (B)describe the kinds of commercial news, such as distant prices and harvest failures, that merchants exchanged when they met each year.
  3. (C)explain how permanent shops in growing towns gradually took away the retail trade on which the great periodic fairs had always depended.
  4. (D)argue that the great fairs mattered chiefly as fixed meetings for settling credit and sharing news, functions that the view of them as large markets overlooks.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 1 says the market picture 'describes the visible part of the fair and mistakes it for the whole'. Paragraphs 2 and 3 describe the two hidden functions, settling credit and exchanging news, and paragraph 4 reinterprets the decline of fairs in their light. The purpose is to relocate the fair's importance.

Why not (A): Paragraph 1 says the market account 'is not wrong' and paragraph 4 says fairs continued to 'matter as markets', so denying that they were ever important as markets contradicts the author.

Why not (B): The news of paragraph 3 is one of two functions the author uses to support the thesis; the option takes a single supporting paragraph for the purpose of the whole.

Why not (C): This is the conventional account the author treats as incomplete; paragraph 4 calls the shift of retail business real but says 'the deeper loss' lay elsewhere.

Common misconception: Overstating the correction into a denial that fairs were markets, or choosing one supporting paragraph.

Difficulty: The thesis is announced in paragraph 1 and restated in paragraph 4; the other options are clearly too narrow or too extreme. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-042 · Everyday life in history · passage Easy · 445 words · 4 questions

Few ideas feel more natural than the distinction between home and work. We leave one for the other in the morning and return in the evening, and much of our moral vocabulary depends on the contrast: home is where we are valued for who we are, work where we are valued for what we produce. Yet for most of recorded history, in most agrarian and artisan societies, the distinction would have puzzled the people who lived in households. The house was where the work was done.

Consider a weaver's dwelling in a pre-industrial town, or a farmstead in the countryside. The looms or the dairy stood beside the hearth, and the people who ate at the table included not only parents and children but apprentices, servants and labourers who lived under the same roof and the same authority. The head of the household was at once father, employer and, in a limited sense, magistrate, answerable to the wider community for the conduct of everyone in his charge. Records of such households often used the word family to mean this whole working unit, and a reader who assumes that it meant blood relatives alone will misread them badly. Children worked as soon as they were able, carding wool or minding animals, and learning a trade was part of growing up in the house rather than something that began when a child left it.

None of this should be romanticised. The working household was a hierarchy, often a harsh one, in which servants could be beaten and wives had little legal standing apart from their husbands. Nor was it universal: the poorest labourers, who owned no tools and rented a single room, had little to bring home but their wages. The point is not that the old arrangement was better, but that it was different in kind, and that the difference matters for how we read later history.

When production moved into workshops, mills and offices, the household did not simply lose its economic role; it acquired a new meaning. Freed of the loom and the dairy, it could be imagined as a private refuge, governed by affection rather than discipline, and this ideal was then projected backwards as if it had always existed. The ideal was real enough for those who could afford it, but it was an ideal, not a description of the past. Laments about the decline of the family often rest on that projection. What changed over these centuries was less the strength of family feeling than the range of tasks the household was expected to perform, and a history that confuses the two will find decline where there was mainly transformation.

  4. (D)the whole group living and working under the household head, servants and apprentices included.

CAT-VARC-P04-043 · Everyday life in history · passage Medium · 454 words · 4 questions

Before the railways, every town kept its own time. Noon was the moment the sun stood highest over the local meridian, and because the sun appears to move westward, a town some distance to the west of another reached its noon a few minutes later. Nobody minded, because nothing travelled fast enough for the difference to matter; a coach journey of several days absorbed a quarter of an hour's discrepancy without anyone noticing it. The railway changed this, and the standard account of what followed is a story of practical necessity. Timetables drawn up in dozens of local times produced confusion and, on single-track lines, danger, so the companies adopted a single reference time and governments eventually followed.

The account is true as far as it goes, but it treats the change as a technical adjustment, like agreeing on a common gauge for the rails. It was more than that. Local time had been public in a literal sense: anyone could see the sun, and the clock on the church or the town hall was set by an observation that a curious citizen could, in principle, check. Standard time was set somewhere else, by astronomers in a distant observatory, and reached the town along a telegraph wire. The hour on the station clock was no longer a fact about the place where one stood but a decision taken elsewhere and transmitted to it.

This helps to explain a resistance that is usually remembered only as quaint. Some towns kept two clocks running side by side, or fitted a public dial with an extra hand so that the sun's reckoning and the railway's could be read together, and some local worthies denounced the new hour as an imposition. It is easy to read them as people clinging to habit in the face of progress. But they had grasped something that the language of convenience obscures: a shared standard is also a centre of authority, and agreeing to it means accepting that the definition of an everyday reality will henceforth be made by people one will never meet.

None of this is a case against standard time, whose benefits were real and soon taken for granted. Few people today would wish to return to a world in which the hour changed as one rode across a county. The argument is rather that the history of timekeeping is also a history of who is entitled to say what time it is, and that this question did not disappear when it was settled. Each later standard, from time signals broadcast by radio to the clocks that now synchronise themselves silently over networks, has extended the same arrangement, and each has made it a little harder to notice.

  3. (C)church authorities in each town insisted that the local hour be kept and discouraged comparison with other places.
  4. (D)few people owned clocks, so the hour of noon was a matter of rough guesswork in every town alike.

Paragraph 1: 'Nobody minded, because nothing travelled fast enough for the difference to matter', and a several-day coach journey 'absorbed a quarter of an hour's discrepancy'.

Common misconception: Supplying a plausible historical reason in place of the one the passage gives.

Why not (B): This captures only the standard account of paragraph 1, a practical fix for confusion; it leaves out what paragraph 2 adds, that the standard's source moved out of local reach.

Why not (C): Here the standard remains local and checkable by anyone; accuracy improves but authority does not move, which is the opposite of paragraph 2.

Common misconception: Choosing the option about resolving confusion, which matches the familiar account but not the specific change paragraph 2 identifies.

Difficulty: The most tempting wrong option fits the passage's topic and the standard account; only the correct answer carries the shift from checkable to remote authority. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P04-044 · Everyday life in history · passage Medium · 434 words · 4 questions

A familiar story about the history of reading holds that, for most of antiquity and much of the Middle Ages, people could read only aloud, and that silent reading was a late discovery which transformed the inner life of the West. The story is usually supported by a much-cited anecdote in which an ancient writer records his surprise at finding a learned man reading without voicing the words. It is a vivid story, and like many vivid stories it has been asked to carry more weight than it can bear.

Scholars who have examined the evidence more closely have found a fair number of ancient readers who read silently, and they point out that surprise at a practice is not evidence that it was unknown, only that it was unusual where it was observed. The more defensible claim is not that silent reading was impossible but that it was not the default. Texts were frequently written without spaces between words, which made voicing them a useful aid, and, more importantly, reading was often a social act. A household, a monastery or a circle of friends would gather while one person read to the others; a book was something one heard as often as something one saw.

What changed, then, was less the technique than the setting. As books became cheaper and more numerous, as spaces between words and other aids to the eye became standard, and as people of moderate means came to have rooms, or at least corners, to themselves, reading moved from the common table to the private chair. Freed from the pace of the voice, the eye could also move faster, and a reader alone could get through more books, and more kinds of book, than a listener ever could. The shift was gradual and uneven, and reading aloud never vanished. Families read novels to one another well into the age of print, and workers in some trades paid a colleague to read to them while they laboured.

Even corrected in this way, the history matters. A text that is heard together is interpreted together: listeners react, object and argue, and the meaning of a passage is settled, if it is settled at all, in company. A text read alone is answerable only to its reader, who may skip, reread or linger over a sentence that no one else will ever know about. It would be too much to say that private reading created the private self. It would be too little to say that it changed nothing about how people came to think of their thoughts as their own.

  3. (C)most readers had learned their letters by reciting them aloud to a teacher in a group.
  4. (D)books were so expensive that a single copy had to be shared among many listeners at once.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Why not (A): No such attitude is described; the anecdote of surprise concerns unfamiliarity, not discourtesy.

Why not (C): The passage says nothing about how readers were taught; the aid it names is the absence of word spacing.

Why not (D): Cost and shared reading are mentioned, but as reasons for the social setting, not as the reason voicing helped a reader decode a text.

Common misconception: Picking a reason connected with the social setting rather than the one tied to voicing as an aid.

Difficulty: One sentence in paragraph 2 gives the answer directly. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-045 · Everyday life in history · passage Medium · 416 words · 4 questions

It is easy to write the history of urban smell as a simple story of progress. Pre-modern cities stank of sewage, tanneries, slaughterhouses and unwashed bodies; reformers built drains and water supplies; the stench retreated. Every part of this is true, and anyone inclined to romanticise the crowded streets of earlier centuries should be reminded of it. But the story assumes that the nose is a constant instrument, registering the same smells with the same disgust in every age, and that assumption does not survive a reading of the sources.

Residents of older towns certainly complained about smells, but their complaints were selective. Odours that later generations would find unbearable, such as the smell of a dung heap beside a house, were often accepted as part of ordinary life, while others, associated with particular trades or with neighbours who were disliked, drew sustained protest. What made a smell intolerable depended less on its strength than on what it was thought to mean. And from the late eighteenth century onwards, in much of Europe, smells came to carry a very specific meaning with new urgency. Under the old theory that disease spread through foul air, now taken up by physicians and reformers, a bad smell was not merely unpleasant; it was a warning, perhaps even the danger itself.

The theory was wrong, but it was productive. Because it identified smell with sickness, it gave reformers a reason, and a sense of urgency, to remove the sources of smell, and many of the measures they took, from covered sewers to the regular removal of refuse, did reduce disease, though for reasons they did not understand. A mistaken idea about how illness spread thus helped to justify works whose real benefit lay elsewhere. When the germ theory displaced it, smell lost its medical authority; it did not lose its power.

Freed from medicine, the vocabulary of smell attached itself more firmly to persons. Middle-class households that had learned to fear foul air now learned to fear the odour of the poor, the newcomer and the manual worker, and the judgement passed easily from bodies to character. The cleaner cities became, the more a lingering smell could serve as a mark of social distance. This is why a history of smell cannot be only a history of drains; it must also be a history of who was allowed to call whom offensive, and of how a sense that seems the most immediate of all has always been schooled.

  3. (C)was stronger than the ordinary smells of household refuse and animal dung that residents lived beside.
  4. (D)came from the drains and sewers that reformers had recently built to carry waste away from the streets.

Common misconception: Assuming that stronger smells drew the most complaint.

CAT-VARC-P04-046 · Everyday life in history · passage Medium · 426 words · 4 questions

The phrase most people use for the overland trade between China and the Mediterranean is a nineteenth-century coinage, and it carries a picture with it: a single road, and on it long caravans of camels carrying silk from one end of Asia to the other. The picture is attractive and largely misleading, for very few goods, and fewer merchants, made the whole journey. What existed instead was a chain of overlapping regional circuits, each worked by traders who knew their own stretch of desert, mountain and oasis intimately and knew little of what lay beyond it.

In such a system a bale of silk might change hands many times between the place where it was woven and the place where it was worn. Each exchange added a margin, which is one reason why goods from the far end of the chain were so costly, and each was made between people who had reason to trust one another because they met repeatedly. The merchant in an oasis town did not need to know the distant market where the silk would end up; he needed to know the caravan leader who would buy it from him and the one who had sold it to him. Long-distance trade, on this view, was an accumulation of short-distance relationships.

This has consequences that the romantic image hides. First, the system was more resilient than a single road would have been: when war or banditry closed one segment, goods could often find a way round it through neighbouring circuits, and trade slowed rather than stopped. Second, knowledge travelled poorly along the chain even when goods travelled well, and buyers at one end often held fanciful beliefs about where their luxuries came from, because no one they dealt with had seen the source. Third, those who profited most were frequently neither the producers nor the final buyers but the middlemen at the junctions where circuits met, whose cities grew rich on the margin.

It would be wrong to conclude that no one crossed the whole distance. Pilgrims, envoys and a few adventurous traders made extraordinary journeys, and some left accounts that are still read. But these travellers are remembered precisely because they were exceptional, and their fame has shaped our picture of the ordinary trade far more than the ordinary trade itself, which left few memoirs. A history built on the exceptional journey will see a road; a history built on the routine exchange will see a web, and it is the web that explains how the trade actually worked.

CAT-VARC-P04-047 · Everyday life in history · passage Difficult · 593 words · 5 questions

Two stories are commonly told about the measures that late medieval and early modern cities took against plague. In the first, the health boards of the Italian city-states, with their isolation hospitals, their passes for travellers and their periods of enforced seclusion for ships and households, are the ancestors of modern public health: rational officials confronting an epidemic with the tools of an emerging state. In the second, the same measures are a chapter in the history of coercion, in which the sick and the poor were locked away, their goods burned and their livelihoods destroyed by authorities who feared contagion more than they valued the people who might carry it. Each story can be furnished with abundant evidence. What both tend to overlook is that the measures could work only with a degree of cooperation that neither officials nor force could guarantee.

Quarantine depends, at its most basic, on information. A household must be shut up before the disease spreads from it, which means that someone must report the first case, and the people best placed to report it are the members of the household themselves, together with their neighbours. Every one of them had reasons to stay silent. A shut household lost its income; its members might be removed to an isolation hospital from which many did not return; its bedding and clothing might be burned. Families that concealed a death, bribed an inspector or slipped out of the city before the doors were sealed were not simply defying authority. They were responding sensibly to the costs that reporting imposed on them, and neighbours who might have informed on them faced, in their turn, the prospect of being shut up beside them.

Where the measures had any success, it seems to have been because authorities, sometimes reluctantly, took on obligations in return. Some cities fed shut-up households at public expense, paid compensation for goods that had been destroyed, or relied on clergy and charitable brotherhoods to bring food, relief and reassurance to the confined. Such provisions were expensive and often inadequate, and the surviving records do not allow us to say with any confidence how much difference they made to mortality. But they changed the calculation facing a family that suspected illness, and the complaints of officials who lacked the means to provide them suggest that concealment rose when relief failed.

It might be objected that this underrates force. Cities did post guards, punish concealment severely and, on occasion, execute those who broke quarantine. Yet harshness had a cost that its advocates rarely counted: the more frightening the consequences of discovery, the stronger the incentive not to be discovered, and an epidemic that goes underground is harder, not easier, to contain. A household that expected to be sealed in with its sick, guarded and fined had every reason to wait, to hide the first symptoms and to hope, and each day of waiting was a day in which the disease could travel. Severity could secure compliance from those already known to the authorities; it could not make the unknown come forward.

The lesson is not that plague regulations were humane, for many plainly were not, nor that they were futile. It is that measures which depend on people revealing something about themselves are bargains, whether or not those who impose them think of them in that way, and the point is not confined to plague. Their success is limited by what disclosure costs the person who discloses, and an authority that wishes to know must first make knowing bearable.

CAT-VARC-P04-047-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
Which of the following does the passage mention as a way in which some cities reduced the burden that quarantine placed on households?
  1. (A)Feeding shut-up households at public expense and compensating them for goods that had been destroyed.
  2. (B)Allowing households that reported a case promptly to leave the city before the doors were sealed.
  3. (C)Excusing households that reported a case from having their bedding and clothing burned.
  4. (D)Rewarding neighbours who informed on a household that had concealed a death from the inspectors.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 3: 'Some cities fed shut-up households at public expense, paid compensation for goods that had been destroyed, or relied on clergy and charitable brotherhoods'.

Why not (B): Slipping out of the city appears in paragraph 2 as a form of evasion, not as something authorities allowed.

Why not (C): Burning goods is listed among the costs of reporting; the passage says cities paid compensation, not that they waived destruction.

Why not (D): No reward for informers is mentioned; paragraph 2 says neighbours themselves had reasons to stay silent.

Common misconception: Choosing an option built from words in paragraph 2 that describe evasion or costs rather than relief.

Difficulty: The relief measures are listed in one sentence of paragraph 3. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-047-Q2 · Main idea / primary purpose · MediumMCQ
Which of the following best states the central claim of the passage?
  1. (A)Modern health authorities should provide generous relief to quarantined households, since history shows that coercion fails to contain disease.
  2. (B)Harsh punishment of concealment drove plague underground, and this is why the measures taken by early modern cities usually failed.
  3. (C)Plague measures relied on people reporting illness, so their success depended on how far authorities reduced what reporting cost.
  4. (D)Plague measures in early modern cities were essentially coercive, and their apparent humanity has been exaggerated by historians of public health.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 1 says both standard stories overlook cooperation; paragraphs 2 to 4 show that quarantine needed information from households, that relief changed their calculation and that severity deterred disclosure; paragraph 5 generalises that such measures 'are bargains' limited by 'what disclosure costs'.

Why not (A): The passage draws a general lesson about disclosure but offers no prescription for modern authorities and does not say coercion simply fails.

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 makes this point about force, but paragraph 5 denies the measures were 'futile'; the option takes one paragraph for the whole and overstates it.

Why not (D): This is the second of the two stories in paragraph 1, both of which, the author says, 'tend to overlook' the role of cooperation; it is reported, not adopted.

Common misconception: Picking the reported coercion story, or the narrower point about force.

Difficulty: The wrong options use real material from the passage but are too narrow, reported views, or prescriptive. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-047-Q3 · Meaning in context · MediumMCQ
In the final sentence, the phrase 'make knowing bearable' most nearly refers to
  1. (A)limiting what authorities try to find out about households, so that people are not burdened by intrusive questioning.
  2. (B)lowering the price that people who reveal illness must pay, so that the information the authority needs reaches it.
  3. (C)helping officials to cope with the distress of learning how widely an epidemic had already spread through the city.
  4. (D)teaching the public enough about how disease spreads that the facts of an epidemic no longer seem frightening to them.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

The preceding clause says success 'is limited by what disclosure costs the person who discloses'. For the authority to know, disclosure must become bearable for the discloser, which is what relief did in paragraph 3.

Why not (A): The sentence speaks of an authority 'that wishes to know'; the remedy is to lower the cost of disclosure, not to ask less.

Why not (C): The phrase concerns the cost of disclosure to 'the person who discloses', not the feelings of the officials who receive the news.

Why not (D): The passage is about the costs of reporting, not public understanding of disease; education is never discussed.

Common misconception: Attaching 'bearable' to the wrong party, the officials or the public at large.

Difficulty: The phrase is compressed; its referent must be fixed from the clause before it and from paragraph 3. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-047-Q4 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following, if true, would most seriously weaken the argument of paragraph 4?
  1. (A)Where penalties for concealment were sharply raised, early reports of cases increased, and no later rise in hidden deaths came to light.
  2. (B)Cities that imposed severe penalties for concealment usually provided relief to shut-up households at the same time.
  3. (C)Guards posted outside shut-up households were frequently bribed to let members of the household leave at night.
  4. (D)Cities that punished concealment most severely also recorded some of the highest plague mortality of any cities in their region during the same period.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 4 argues that severity 'could not make the unknown come forward' because fear of discovery strengthens the incentive to hide. Evidence that harsher penalties produced more early reporting without a rise in hidden deaths directly contradicts this mechanism.

Why not (B): This makes it hard to separate the effects of force and relief, but it gives no evidence that severity actually brought hidden cases to light.

Why not (C): Bribery of guards shows force failing in practice, which fits the author's scepticism about force rather than undermining it.

Why not (D): High mortality in harsh cities is consistent with the author's claim that severity drives an epidemic underground, so it does not weaken it.

Common misconception: Choosing an option that is unfavourable to plague regulation in general but actually agrees with the author's view of force.

Difficulty: Two wrong options are on topic and seem critical of force, but both support the paragraph; the confound option is tempting but neutral. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P04-047-Q5 · Inference · DifficultMCQ
It can be inferred that the author regards the evidence about relief discussed in paragraph 3 as
  1. (A)conclusive proof that public relief lowered plague mortality in the cities that were able to afford it.
  2. (B)too unreliable to support any conclusion at all, since it comes mainly from the complaints of officials.
  3. (C)suggestive about relief's effect on whether families concealed illness, but insufficient to show its effect on the death toll.
  4. (D)evidence that relief was generous enough to remove the costs of reporting for most of the households affected.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 3 separates two questions: the effect on mortality, which the records cannot settle, and the effect on concealment, which officials' complaints 'suggest'. The correct answer keeps both halves of that distinction.

Why not (A): The author says the records 'do not allow us to say with any confidence how much difference they made to mortality', which rules out conclusive proof.

Why not (B): The author does draw a conclusion from the officials' complaints, that 'concealment rose when relief failed', so the evidence is not dismissed.

Why not (D): Paragraph 3 calls the provisions 'expensive and often inadequate', so the author does not think they removed the costs of reporting.

Common misconception: Merging the two questions, and so treating the evidence as either decisive or worthless.

Difficulty: Hinges on the distinction between mortality and concealment within one paragraph; two wrong options each collapse it in opposite directions. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P04-048 · Everyday life in history · passage Difficult · 584 words · 5 questions

The usual history of artificial light is a history of liberation. For most of human existence the coming of darkness set a hard limit on what people could do: work stopped, travel became dangerous, and the town shrank to the circle of the hearth. Oil lamps, then gas, then electricity pushed that limit back, until the night became little more than a dimmer continuation of the day, available for labour, commerce and pleasure. The story is not false. But it treats the night as an absence, a gap waiting to be filled, and in doing so it forgets that the pre-industrial night was a time with its own rules, its own inhabitants and its own uses.

In many European towns before public lighting, the night was governed differently from the day. Gates were shut, curfew bells were rung, and anyone abroad after dark without a lantern and a good reason might be stopped and questioned by the watch. The respectable stayed indoors; the streets belonged to watchmen, to those whose trades required darkness, to lovers, thieves and the young. This was not freedom in any simple sense, for the dark was also where violence went unpunished. It was, however, a separate jurisdiction, a time in which the authority of the day ran only weakly and in which much that could not be done openly was done.

The first systematic street lighting was installed less to lengthen the working day than to bring this jurisdiction under control. Its sponsors spoke of safety, and they meant it, but safety meant visibility, and visibility meant that the street at midnight could be watched and policed by the same standards as the street at noon. Accounts from several cities describe crowds breaking lamps in moments of unrest, and it is tempting to dismiss this as mere vandalism. It is more plausibly read as a recognition, however inarticulate, that the lamps were an extension of an authority that the crowd had reason to resist. The lamp, in this sense, was an instrument of the day carried into the night.

Yet to replace the story of liberation with a story of discipline would be to make the opposite mistake. Lighting did extend surveillance, but it also opened the night to people who had been effectively shut out of it. Respectable women, families, the elderly and the cautious could now walk to a theatre or a shop after dark, and whole new forms of sociability, from evening promenades to late markets, grew up in the lit streets. For many people the expansion of the policed night was, quite simply, an expansion of their world. The same lamp that exposed the thief to the watch made the street usable for the seamstress walking home.

What lighting did, then, was neither simply to free the night nor simply to conquer it, but to abolish it as a distinct social time. The day's rules, the day's people and the day's activities spread into hours that had once belonged to others. Those who had held the dark, the watchmen, the night trades and the young, lost not their lives but their hours, and the newcomers to the lit street rarely noticed what had been displaced. Whether we count this as a gain or a loss depends largely on whom we imagine walking the street, and it is precisely the habit of imagining only one kind of walker that has made the history of light so easy to tell and so hard to tell well.

CAT-VARC-P04-048-Q1 · Structure / function · MediumMCQ
Which of the following best describes the role of paragraph 4 in the passage?
  1. (A)It returns to the history of liberation described in paragraph 1, which the author now accepts, after all, as the correct and complete account.
  2. (B)It offers evidence that the crowds who broke lamps in paragraph 3 were acting against the interests of most townspeople.
  3. (C)It withdraws the claim of paragraph 3 that street lighting extended policing, conceding that safety was the sponsors' only real aim.
  4. (D)It keeps the critique of paragraph 3 from becoming a new one-sided story, showing that lighting also widened access to the night.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 4 opens: 'to replace the story of liberation with a story of discipline would be to make the opposite mistake', and then describes women, families and the elderly gaining the night. It balances paragraph 3 before the synthesis of paragraph 5.

Why not (A): The paragraph calls replacing one story with the other 'the opposite mistake', and paragraph 5 rejects both simple stories.

Why not (B): The paragraph does not mention the crowds or judge their interests; it describes groups who gained access to the night.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 says 'Lighting did extend surveillance'; it adds a second effect without withdrawing the first.

Common misconception: Reading a balancing paragraph as a retraction of what came before.

Difficulty: The paragraph's role is signalled by its first sentence, but two wrong options misread it as retraction or reversal. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-048-Q2 · Meaning in context · MediumMCQ
As used in paragraph 2, the phrase 'a separate jurisdiction' most nearly means
  1. (A)a legal code written specifically for offences committed after dark, with harsher penalties than by day.
  2. (B)a zone of complete freedom in which no rules of any kind applied to those who were out after dark.
  3. (C)a period in which daytime authority held only loosely and different customs and people governed the street.
  4. (D)a district of the town placed under the control of the night watch rather than of the ordinary magistrates.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

The phrase is glossed in the same sentence: 'a time in which the authority of the day ran only weakly and in which much that could not be done openly was done'. Paragraph 1 adds that the night had 'its own rules, its own inhabitants'.

Why not (A): No special code is described; the passage says violence in the dark often 'went unpunished', the reverse of harsher penalties.

Why not (B): Paragraph 1 says the night had 'its own rules', paragraph 2 describes curfews and questioning by the watch, and the dark 'was not freedom in any simple sense'.

Why not (D): The passage speaks of a time, not a place: 'a time in which the authority of the day ran only weakly'.

Common misconception: Taking 'jurisdiction' literally as a place or a legal code, or as a total absence of rules.

Difficulty: The figurative sense must be read off the gloss and squared with the curfew details. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P04-048-Q3 · Inference · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following best describes how the author interprets the crowds who broke street lamps?
  1. (A)As probably sensing, even if they could not say so, that the lamps carried an authority they had grounds to oppose.
  2. (B)As vandals whose actions have been wrongly dignified by historians eager to find political resistance.
  3. (C)As giving clear proof that most townspeople opposed street lighting from the moment it was installed.
  4. (D)As defending the freedom that thieves and other night dwellers had enjoyed before the lamps were put up.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 3: the lamp-breaking 'is more plausibly read as a recognition, however inarticulate, that the lamps were an extension of an authority that the crowd had reason to resist'. The correct answer keeps the hedge ('more plausibly', 'however inarticulate').

Why not (B): The author says it is 'tempting to dismiss this as mere vandalism' and then rejects that reading as less plausible.

Why not (C): The author offers a hedged reading of some crowds 'in moments of unrest'; paragraph 4 adds that many people gained from lighting.

Why not (D): The author does not say the crowds acted for thieves; the reading offered concerns resistance to authority, not any particular group's interests.

Common misconception: Upgrading the author's hedged reading into a certainty, or attributing to the author the dismissal the author rejects.

Difficulty: The wrong options differ from the correct answer mainly in degree and in whose interests are named; the hedge must be noticed. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P04-048-Q4 · Application / analogy · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following is most closely analogous to what the author says, in paragraph 5, that lighting did to the night?
  1. (A)A town holds a festival one night a year at which ordinary rules are suspended and people may behave as they never would by day.
  2. (B)A public library extends its opening hours into the evening so that its regular readers can stay and read for longer after work.
  3. (C)A council installs cameras in an empty square that nobody had used at night, so that the area can be monitored by the police.
  4. (D)A beach once used at dawn only by fishermen, under their own customs, becomes a public promenade with council rules, drawing families but ending the fishermen's ways.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 5 says lighting abolished the night 'as a distinct social time': 'the day's rules, the day's people and the day's activities spread into hours that had once belonged to others'. The beach case has a distinct prior use with its own customs, an extension of general rules, new users who gain, and the old users' ways lost.

Why not (A): This creates a distinct time with its own rules, which is the reverse of abolishing one.

Why not (B): This lengthens an existing activity for the same users under the same rules; it captures only the liberation story, not the replacement of a distinct time.

Why not (C): This captures surveillance only, and the square had no prior users or customs to displace, so there is no distinct time being abolished.

Common misconception: Choosing an analogy for only one of the two stories the author rejects (liberation or discipline).

Difficulty: Each wrong option models one partial reading; the correct answer requires all elements of the paragraph 5 synthesis. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P04-048-Q5 · Inference · DifficultMCQ
The final sentence of the passage implies that the usual histories of light have gone wrong chiefly because they
  1. (A)neglect to measure how far lighting actually lengthened the hours that people spent working, travelling and shopping.
  2. (B)see the street through one kind of user's eyes, missing that a single change brought gain to some and loss to others.
  3. (C)count lighting as a gain when, properly understood, its effects on everyday life were very largely a loss for townspeople.
  4. (D)depend on the testimony of the officials who installed the lamps, and so repeat those officials' claims about safety without ever questioning them.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

The sentence says the verdict depends on 'whom we imagine walking the street', and that 'the habit of imagining only one kind of walker' made the history easy to tell badly. The flaw is a single standpoint that hides divided effects.

Why not (A): The author's complaint is about whose experience is imagined, not about missing measurements of hours.

Why not (C): The author declines to call it simply gain or loss, saying the verdict 'depends largely on whom we imagine walking the street'.

Why not (D): The passage notes that sponsors 'spoke of safety, and they meant it', but it never says historians relied on their testimony.

Common misconception: Taking the author to have a hidden verdict (loss), when the point is that the verdict varies by user.

Difficulty: The compressed final sentence must be unpacked; one option smuggles in a verdict the author refuses to give. · Exams: CAT, XAT
